Samsung’s leaked Galaxy S27 pricing reveals a four-tiered lineup that locks top displays behind Pro paywalls.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Samsung Galaxy S27 lineup could be a relief for shoppers worried about another round of flagship price hikes, but the good news may come with a catch. A fresh leak indicates the company will introduce four models in early 2027, with more sophisticated OLED displays reserved for its two priciest phones.
According to pricing details shared by tipster @phonefuturist on X, the Galaxy S27 could start at $899 in the US. The S27 Plus may cost $1,099, followed by the S27 Pro at $1,299 and the S27 Ultra at $1,399. Pricing is expected to vary internationally, with the Ultra potentially priced as high as $1,499 depending on configuration.
🚨 EXCLUSIVE: Galaxy S27 series pricing
🇺🇸 US/ 🇰🇷SK | 🌍 Int'l*
S27: $899 | $999 — M14+
S27+: $1,099 | $1,199 — M14+
S27 Pro: $1,299 | $1,399 — M16
S27 Ultra: $1,399/$1,499 | $1,499–$1,549 — M16
Regional pricing varies. Current targets, subject to change.October 7, 2026
The leaked lineup would be a change for Samsung, which may be adding a Pro model between the Plus and Ultra. The company, though, has not confirmed the phones or their prices, and the tipster describes the figures as preliminary targets.
The more interesting detail is the split display. The regular S27 and S27 Plus are said to come with Samsung’s M14+ OLED panels, while the Pro and Ultra are said to have the newer M16 panels. That difference could see the privacy-display tech included on the more expensive models, while the cheaper-phone buyers miss out.
There is also a financial reason to be cautious. If prices rise, Samsung may have to tweak them pre-launch, possibly negating the perceived savings on the entry-level model. And here’s the awkward flip side: Samsung’s chip business is enjoying a boost from surging AI demand and tight memory supply — but those same market pressures could make its next phones more difficult for regular buyers to afford.
As someone who wants a reliable phone without spending over $1,000, I’d welcome a cheaper standard Galaxy S27. But I’d be annoyed if good screen tech were used as an excuse to push buyers toward more expensive models. Most people shouldn’t have to pay Ultra money to get a premium everyday experience.
These numbers could change as Samsung's next-generation flagship is still months away. Until the company makes an official announcement, buyers should consider the leak as an early pricing guide.

Hurricane Isaias made landfall Friday night near Destin, Florida, as a Category 2 hurricane, packing 105 mph winds.
The National Hurricane Center said ""life-threatening storm surge and devastating winds"" were likely along the Florida Panhandle and Alabama coasts. The coastline from the Alabama-Florida border east to Grayton Beach, Fla., was expected to see 6 to 9 feet of water, according to the hurricane center. A broader area was forecast to see a surge of more than 4 feet.
Widespread power outages are expected, along with tornadoes and flash flooding as the storm moves inland Saturday. Rainfall totals over the weekend could reach 4 to 8 inches in parts of Alabama, Florida and Georgia, with up to 15 inches possible in some areas.
Isaias is the first hurricane of the Atlantic season, which has until now been dampened by a strong El Nino , a naturally occurring climate pattern. It is the first hurricane since 2024 to hit the U.S.
The storm is bringing strong winds, heavy rains, and threats of a deep storm surge to the northern Gulf Coast, the National Hurricane Center said.
""This is a life-threatening situation,"" the National Hurricane Center said in its advisory, urging people to protect lives and property, and to follow evacuation orders.
Forecasters say wind conditions should start to weaken after landfall, with Isaias expected to be downgraded to a post-tropical storm on Saturday.
Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis on Friday evening warned residents in the hurricane's path and beyond to prepare for heavy rain and potential tornadoes.
""Assume you're going to lose power,"" he said at a news conference.
As of about 9 p.m., more than 460,000 customers had lost power across Alabama, Florida and Georgia, according to PowerOutage.US.

                Gina Holland evacuates her home with her dog Cici in Pensacola, Fla., before the expected landfall of Hurricane Isaias.
                
                    
                    Joe Raedle/Getty Images
                    
                
hide caption

The governor earlier stressed that while well-designed buildings can withstand high winds, floodwaters present a serious threat to lives and property.
""We always say, you know, you hide from the wind, but you run from the water,"" DeSantis told reporters Friday morning. ""When the water's rising and things are happening, it's a very, very difficult conundrum, and you do not want to be caught in that.""
A hurricane warning extended along the Gulf Coast from Ocean Springs, Miss., eastward to the area around Mexico Beach in Florida.
Rainfall totals over the weekend could reach 4 to 8 inches in parts of Alabama, Florida and Georgia, with up to 15 inches possible in some areas. 
Flash floods are a certainty, according to Jared Perdue, the executive director of Florida's Division of Emergency Management. ""The Panhandle is already saturated from rainfall,"" he said at Friday morning's press conference alongside DeSantis. ""This storm will bring heavy amounts of rain … there will be flash flooding, there will be localized flooding.""
DeSantis said that some 16,000 utility workers are standing by to help restore electricity service. The Florida National Guard also will have approximately 5,000 personnel available to help the recovery effort, he said.

                Shelves were empty of bread in a Walmart store in Gulf Breeze, Fla., on Thursday, as people stocked up ahead of the arrival of Hurricane Isaias.
                
                    
                    Joe Raedle/Getty Images
                    
                
hide caption

In the Florida Panhandle, many schools and commercial airports were shut down, and mandatory or voluntary evacuation orders were in effect for parts of seven counties as of Friday morning. In Alabama, Gov. Kay Ivey declared a state of emergency for 40 counties; DeSantis did the same for 25 counties.
It has been more than 100 years since the Atlantic got its first hurricane of the season this late, as a ""Super El Niño"" weather pattern has disrupted the ability of cyclones to form. But the pattern has done less to affect storm formation in the Gulf, where very warm water has served as fuel for storms like Isaias.
Warm ocean water helps tropical storms build strength and draw in moisture, adding to their potential rainfall. Warmer sea surface temperatures are also linked to human-caused climate change, a critical factor in making hurricanes larger and more likely to bring intense winds and rainfall when they make landfall.

26,2026-10-09,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Rebecca Paxton, the 35-year-old cofounder and managing partner of My Front Porch, a Dallas pumpkin-display business. It's been edited for length and clarity.
I was working in corporate America at a Fortune 100 company with my cofounder, Jen Carmichael, when we got the idea for My Front Porch. We'd worked together in HR for 10 years, but life changes made us realize we needed more flexibility.
One day in 2022, Jen saw her neighbor with a pumpkin display and asked who'd set it up. Her neighbor's daughter had ordered it for her from a local business. Jen was interested in ordering a display for her home, but when she went to the website, they were already sold out for the year.
She came to the office and told me about it, and the idea of starting our own business became kind of a joke between us. We'd be working late nights in the office, talking about how we'd rather be outside decorating. It sounded so much more fun than going through spreadsheets and complex business problems.
In late 2024, we both left our jobs. I had just had my second baby, and Jen's father had recently died, so she needed to step away to help her family. In January 2025, we decided to launch the business.
I also wanted to be a career woman. It's hard to do that in the corporate world.
We set up our LLC, operating agreement, and bank account. We also got our website up and running, started our social media accounts, and began figuring out our supply chain logistics. We took our time to think about every little aspect — from where we'd get our pumpkins to how to deliver them and how best to communicate with customers.
By July 2025, we were taking orders. Pumpkins arrived at our warehouse on September 18, and the next day we began installations.
We have really high standards and expectations, strong problem-solving skills, and a recruiting background, so we knew how to get the right help to staff the business as needed. We also weren't afraid to reach out to our contacts when we didn't know something to get their advice.
One example is the first day we were going to get a big shipment in, we weren't sure how to actually get the pumpkins off the truck and into the storage facility. We didn't really know how to work a pallet jack, so we called a friend who showed us the ropes, and we learned from there.
We brought in semi-truckloads of pumpkins we purchased wholesale, ran multiple trailers with products going out, and rented a climate-controlled storage space.
We originally planned to sell a certain number of displays, but as calls kept coming in during the fall season, we decided to over-order rather than turn customers away. We took that risk, and it was well worth it.
When we set out to do this, we didn't want to do it as a hobby; we wanted to do it at an exceptional level, and at scale, because both of us left high-income jobs and wanted this business to help take care of our families financially. In our first year, we had over $70,000 in sales, and this year we already have over $120,000.
From the outside looking in, people are often surprised by how much complexity goes into the business. We're coordinating installations, putting together routes, and finding suppliers.
Even with the logistics required to operate at scale, we wanted packages for every budget. We have five packages at different price points, including a smaller package than many local competitors offer. Many people don't realize pumpkin-display installation is a service until they see one at a neighbor's house — just as Jen did.
We didn't really have a social media background — we learned along the way. Our main focus was sharing our mission to bring joy across Dallas. We told our story on Instagram, Facebook, TikTok, and Pinterest. We put a lot of effort into defining our brand's aesthetic and what we wanted to represent.
We also have a demographic that's not on Instagram at all, so we did a lot of word-of-mouth outreach. We reached out to friends, asking them to spread the word and put out fliers.
It was crazy — we were getting up and out the door by 6:30 a.m. and coming home between 9:30 and 10 p.m. It's much more physical than what we were used to in the corporate world. During install weeks, we work 60-80 hours. When needed, we've used friends, family, neighbors, and landscaping crews to help.
However, we get a balance because we do most of our work in the three or four months around fall, with all our installations taking place within four weeks.
It's been such a blessing — it's provided the flexibility in our lives that we really needed and given us a way to earn income for our families.
Last year, we chose a few nonprofits to donate displays to and also held a giveaway for a family with pumpkins that had been used at a wedding. This year, we're partnering with the Ronald McDonald House. It's a great opportunity to spread joy in a really intentional and thoughtful way.
We've already thought about expanding — to add additional holidays or operate in other states — but there are over seven million people here in the Dallas market, so we know there's much more market share for us to go after and capture.
The nice thing is we're always willing to pivot. We're agile. Right now, what we're doing feels right, but we're always open to something more.
No comments right now, check back later.

27,2026-10-09,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Christina Qi recently raised $97 million in Series B financing for her data-driven startup. 
Copied to clipboard
Christina Qi boasts an impressive resume. As a 21-year-old undergraduate at MIT, she quietly did something most finance veterans spend decades attempting: She started her own hedge fund. At its height, the fund, Domeyard, traded $7 billion in volume per day. 
In 2020, after eight years of running Domeyard, Qi and her fellow founding partners realized that the fund was not scalable, and performance was suffering. “We had a really bad year in 2020; performance was down 12%, and so we told our investors that we were shutting down,” Qi tells Entrepreneur in a new interview. 
She started fresh that year with a different idea, co-founding and serving as CEO of a new startup called Databento. The company strives to become “the single source of truth for financial information,” Qi explains. It provides market data for researchers at hedge funds and AI labs and now has 70,000 customers, including Nvidia and OpenAI. 
Databento raised $97 million in a Series B funding round in July, bringing the startup’s total disclosed funding to about $127 million. Qi has not made the startup’s valuation public. Earlier this year, Databento’s head of quantitative analytics, Lou Lindley, disclosed that the startup had 8-figure annual recurring revenue.  
“We grew our revenue almost tenfold from a base well above single-digit millions,” Qi says. “We have a lot of competitors in this industry, which is probably why we don’t share exact figures.”
Despite having customers concentrated in Wall Street and Silicon Valley, Qi chooses to reside in her home state of Utah. She runs the business, which employs 38 people, remotely. Instead of seeing coworkers when she steps outside, she looks out at her 10 chickens, two turtles and 15-year-old dog. 
Qi explains her entrepreneurial journey “does feel like the American dream to an extent.” She grew up on food stamps and welfare in a low-income household in Utah before attending MIT. 
“Was it harder? Maybe. Look, the starting line might have been a few steps back,” Qi says. “It might have been a little bit more difficult as a result because of where I’m from.”
She adds that people don’t consider her “CEO material” in social settings.
“When I go to a networking event, people sometimes ask if I can speak English,” Qi says. “Or people think I’m a waitress. I got mistaken for a waitress eight or nine times now in my career. Even two weeks ago, I got mistaken as a waitress at a cafeteria.”
People ask Qi where she is “really from” all the time and aren’t satisfied when she tells them she’s from Utah. “People also say my English is great all the time, but it’s my native language,” she says. 
Qi notes that these experiences were frustrating and demonstrated that she had a “disadvantage” compared to her peers. 
“I still managed to raise a lot of funding, hire a fantastic team and run a tech business selling to quants, who are mostly men in finance,” she says. “I’m in a very male-dominated industry, and I’ve managed to still be fine.”
Qi points out that her story proves it is possible to achieve startup success even when you start at a disadvantage. She hopes to inspire more people to pursue their dreams and be unafraid of starting their own ventures. 
In 2019, Qi was elected co-chair of the board of Invest in Girls, a nonprofit organization focused on financial literacy for high school girls.  She has also held board and committee roles with the MIT Sloan Boston Alumni Association and 100 Women in Finance, a nonprofit that helps women advance their finance careers.
Qi says in the Asian American culture she grew up in, admitting to failure is something to avoid at all costs.
She experienced this firsthand at MIT. As a freshman, she applied to her first internship and received a rejection within 24 hours. “I cried,” Qi says. “I just remember that core memory.”
She applied to 100 internships after that, and then it just became like clockwork. “You realize, Oh, rejection is just normal,” Qi says. “The first time, it hurts the most.” 
Now, Qi considers failure a part of her daily life. Investors and customers reject her every day, and she considers it a part of her job. If she is not facing failure, then she is not doing enough or putting herself out there enough, she says. 
“Part of your job description as a founder, as a CEO, is to put yourself out there, embarrass yourself on stage and get rejected,” Qi says. 
That mindset applied when Qi closed down Domeyard. She wrote in a July 2023 LinkedIn announcement that shutting down the hedge fund “felt like losing a part of my identity.” 
“Domeyard will always be my first venture — letting go was one of the most difficult decisions we’ve ever made,” she wrote. 
Qi said earlier, in a 2020 MIT Sloan interview, that her friends “inspired” her to step away from Domeyard after a “particularly rough year” in 2020. They urged her to take a break and evaluate her career trajectory. 
Databento arose out of that self-reflection. Qi worked up the courage to start the venture despite Domeyard shutting down. She was able to learn from that setback.
Qi also asserts that it is okay to be vulnerable as a founder. She says there’s a certain archetype of founder who is very cutthroat and steps over other founders to obtain funding and attention. Qi encourages founders to follow a different path and be the kind of leader who supports other founders. 
“You can succeed and raise each other up and support each other, even as competitors in this industry,” she says. “That is absolutely possible. That’s what I have done and strive to do with a lot of our competitors in this space, with other founders.”
Christina Qi boasts an impressive resume. As a 21-year-old undergraduate at MIT, she quietly did something most finance veterans spend decades attempting: She started her own hedge fund. At its height, the fund, Domeyard, traded $7 billion in volume per day. 
In 2020, after eight years of running Domeyard, Qi and her fellow founding partners realized that the fund was not scalable, and performance was suffering. “We had a really bad year in 2020; performance was down 12%, and so we told our investors that we were shutting down,” Qi tells Entrepreneur in a new interview. 
She started fresh that year with a different idea, co-founding and serving as CEO of a new startup called Databento. The company strives to become “the single source of truth for financial information,” Qi explains. It provides market data for researchers at hedge funds and AI labs and now has 70,000 customers, including Nvidia and OpenAI.

28,2026-10-09,"Ask most Ryobi owners where the tools come from and you'll hear one answer: Home Depot. It has been the brand's exclusive big-box retailer for decades, and it's where my first drill kit came from. My three cheapest tools came from somewhere else, though. Techtronic Industries, the company that builds Ryobi, runs its own discount store called Direct Tools Factory Outlet, and it sells the same green tools for less than Home Depot charges. 
I bought a handheld tire inflator, a flashlight, and an angle grinder there. None cost full price. Once I understood that the battery was the real investment in this system, shaving money off the tools themselves became the obvious next move, and the outlet is the easiest place I've found to do it.
Techtronic Industries, usually shortened to TTI, makes Ryobi power tools along with Ridgid, Hart, and Hoover products. Direct Tools Factory Outlet is its clearance channel. There's a website, plus physical stores tucked into outlet malls around the country, and both carry stock that can't sit on a regular retail shelf at full price.
That stock falls into two buckets, and the store labels each listing clearly. Nothing arrives from a third-party seller or an anonymous warehouse. Every item comes from the manufacturer's own supply, which matters more than it sounds when you compare it with the marketplace listings most bargain hunters end up scrolling through.
Check these five things to help you determine if a Ryobi power tool battery is fake. 
Factory blemished tools are unused. Something cosmetic kept them out of normal retail, maybe a scratch on the housing or a crushed box, and they ship with the standard manufacturer's warranty. For practical purposes, you're getting a new tool that lost a beauty contest.
Factory reconditioned tools have a history. Someone returned them, and TTI inspected, repaired, and tested each one before listing it again. They carry a one-year warranty instead of the full term, and the outlet warns that kits may arrive with a substitute battery or charger, so the photo isn't a promise.
Buyer reports on reconditioned gear are mixed. Plenty of people get a tool that works like new. Others describe wobbly drill chucks or sanders that behave oddly, which makes sense for anything with wear parts and a previous owner. Blemished is the safer pick for any tool where precision counts.
My handheld tire inflator cost about 80% of its regular retail price, and the flashlight landed at roughly the same discount. The angle grinder was the best deal of the three at around 70% of retail.
Those aren't jaw-dropping numbers, and I won't pretend they are. They add up, though, because of how I buy. I already own a shelf of 18V ONE+ packs, so every one of these was a bare tool with no battery or charger padding the price. A 20% or 30% cut on a tool that was already inexpensive is how something ends up as the cheapest thing hanging in the garage.
The inflator earns its spot more than anything else from that order. It tops off the truck tires, the mower tires, and every ball and pool float that comes through the garage, and it does all of it on whichever compact pack is charged.
Online orders carry a shipping fee, and on a single small tool that fee can erase the discount entirely. Bundling several items into one order, or driving to a store if one is nearby, fixes the math.
Stock is unpredictable. The outlet sells whatever TTI has surplus of, so the exact model you want may not appear for months. Prices need a second look too, because a blemished tool at the outlet sometimes costs more than the same tool during a Home Depot promotion. I check both before buying, the same habit that keeps me from overpaying for batteries.
Timing helps. The outlet runs periodic sales that stack deeper cuts on top of its usual pricing, and that's when the real bargains show up.
For batteries in particular, I'd take a factory outlet over a marketplace listing every time. Cheap packs from unknown sellers are where counterfeit Ryobi batteries turn up, and a pack from the manufacturer's own store removes that worry.
Home Depot still gets most of my Ryobi money, mostly because its holiday kit deals are hard to beat for batteries. For bare tools, I now look at Direct Tools Factory Outlet first. Blemished stock gives you a new tool and a full warranty for less, and reconditioned stock goes lower still if you're comfortable with a shorter warranty and a little risk.
Compare the total with shipping against Home Depot's current price, buy when the outlet runs a sale, and stick to tools that fit batteries you already own. The outlet doesn't carry everything, and one tool I'd never buy from Ryobi at any discount stays off my list.

29,2026-10-09,"Rising fuel prices push Delta’s annual fuel expenses up by $6bn, affecting its profit outlook for 2026.
Save
Share
Delta Air Lines cut its profit forecast despite heightened demand as fuel costs surge across the air travel sector.
The Atlanta-based air carrier said in its third-quarter earnings report released on Friday that it expects its annual fuel costs to jump by $6bn, as ongoing tensions between the United States and Iran have sent fuel prices surging across the global aviation sector.
Overall, US airlines spent nearly $43bn on fuel in the first eight months of the year, a $13.2bn increase from the same period a year earlier.
On Wall Street, Delta cut its earnings outlook for the year to adjusted earnings per share of $5.10 to $5.60, down from its July forecast of $6.50 to $7.50. The midpoint of the new range is below analysts’ average estimate of $5.46, according to LSEG data.
Delta’s stock tumbled in midday trading as the trading week came to a close. It was down 1.1 percent from Friday’s opening price. While the stock has fallen 4.4 percent over the past five days, it has performed well in 2026, gaining nearly 18 percent since the beginning of the year.
CEO Ed Bastian said the airline had raised prices by roughly 20 percent this year and said those prices could be sustained even if fuel prices decline.
Delta Air Lines is the first of the major US airlines to report earnings and is generally more protected from price fluctuations than its competitors because it owns a refinery in Pennsylvania, which it acquired in 2012.
Despite higher prices, demand remains elevated, with 60 percent of flights in the fourth quarter already booked. Delta also announced several new international routes, including Seattle–Tokyo, Japan and Boston–Venice, Italy, as well as Austin-Paris, France, all beginning next year.
Bastian told the Wall Street Journal that holiday bookings also remain strong and that premium travel is growing, with premium seat revenue jumping 18 percent for the quarter compared with the same period last year.
Broadly speaking, premium flights can be a boon for major airlines, and prices in the segment are rising. Average ticket prices for premium seats across major airlines were up 11 percent compared with the same period last year, according to the Airline Reporting Corporation’s monthly report, which tracks airline ticket transactions.
But lower-income consumers are pulling back. US consumer sentiment is slumping, according to the University of Michigan’s Surveys of Consumers, which released its Consumer Sentiment Index on Friday.
“Overall, sentiment for lower-income consumers and those with smaller stock portfolios dropped steeply this month, groups that have fewer resources to weather increases in prices,” Joanne Hsu, director of the Surveys of Consumers, said in the release.
Delta’s report comes as the US summer travel season draws to a close, following reports that lower-income travellers would pull back amid rising prices. In May, 51 percent of Americans earning less than $100,000 annually said travel would be one of the first expenses they would cut, according to a report from consulting firm Deloitte.
United Airlines is the next major carrier expected to report earnings, with results slated for after the market close on October 20. Like Delta, United’s stock was down on Wall Street, falling 0.8 percent from Friday’s opening price.

30,2026-10-09,"Potholes, cracks, and uneven road surfaces are a nuisance at best — and hazardous at worst.
Whether you encounter them occasionally or on your daily commute to work can depend on which state you live in.
Each year, US News & World Report publishes a ranking of the best states to live in, which it calculates from a range of categories, including infrastructure.
As part of its infrastructure ranking, US News & World Report rated the quality of each state's transportation using metrics such as commute time, public transit usage, and road quality.
To evaluate road quality, it used data from the Federal Highway Administration, a division of the US Department of Transportation. The data reflects the percentage of roads in each state rated in poor condition.
The Federal Highway Administration assesses road conditions by examining defects such as cracks, uneven surfaces, and grooves.
US News & World Report used this data to rate each state's transportation quality and, when coupled with energy and internet access, its overall infrastructure ranking.

32,2026-10-09,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
The Michigan Gaming Control Board has issued cease-and-desist letters to 33 offshore operators accused of offering casino games, sweepstakes-style games or sports betting to Michigan residents without a state license. The October action also highlights a gap in protection for people who have self-excluded from legal online gambling.
The MGCB said its investigations found that each operator was offering at least one of the three forms of gambling to Michigan residents without authorization. The named businesses include Azartzona Casino, Basepoker, BingoMania, BitofGold, Cryptoplay, Del Oro Casino, Detective Slots, Donbet, Exclusive Casino, Genie Jackpot, Guangzhou Snail Interactive Technology Co., Ltd., Immersion Casino Online, iWild Casino, JacksPay, Jurassic Slots, L0tt0, Lucky Charm Sweep, Lucky Rebel and Magicianbet.
The remaining operators are mBit Casino, Odds Maker, Raging Bull Slots, Regal Casino and Sportsbook, also known as Wager Street, Ronin Slots, Runewager, Slot Paradise, Sloto Tribe, Sloto’Cash Online Casino, Spin Dinero, SpinsTopia, Stardust, SunnySpins Casino and The Online Casino.
Michigan’s licensed online gambling operators must check the Internet Gaming and Sports Betting Responsible Gaming Database, which allows residents to bar themselves from online gambling. Offshore operators have no obligation to honor that database, so someone who has self-excluded may still be able to open an account, deposit money and place bets on an unlicensed site.
The MGCB warned that people using Michigan’s self-exclusion programs may not realize an offshore site is outside state law. Unlike licensed operators, unlicensed sites are not required to honor Michigan’s self-exclusion system or provide the responsible-gambling protections required by the state.
The board’s warning comes amid broader concerns about illegal gambling. An August 2025 analysis by the American Gaming Association estimated that Americans wager $673.6 billion annually with illegal and unregulated operators, including offshore sites, and said the market had grown 22% since its 2022 report.
The AGA analysis also found that the share of online-casino players using only legal sites fell from 52% in 2022 to 24%. Nearly half of players now use both legal and illegal sites, according to the study. It estimated that Americans placed $84 billion in bets with illegal bookies and offshore sportsbooks, while one in 10 sports bettors wagered exclusively through illegal sources.
Earlier AGA consumer research found that 70% of sports bettors who placed most of their wagers with illegal operators believed they were using only legal sportsbooks or splitting their bets evenly between legal and illegal ones.
The letters add to the MGCB’s ongoing enforcement against illegal online gambling. In April 2026, the board announced that it had sent cease-and-desist letters to 45 offshore operators over a four-month period.
Under Michigan’s Lawful Internet Gaming Act, Lawful Sports Betting Act and related state statutes, companies must be licensed by the MGCB to offer internet gaming or sports wagering in the state. Operators that ignore the letters may face further enforcement action in conjunction with the Michigan Department of Attorney General; the board has not announced additional action against the 33 businesses.
Residents can check authorized online gaming and sports-betting platforms through the Michigan Gaming Control Board website. The agency says illegal gambling websites can be reported by email at [email protected] or through its anonymous tip line at 1-888-314-2682.

33,2026-10-09,"A new Counterpoint Research report says Apple’s next-generation MacBook Pro will help drive a 50% increase in OLED notebook display shipments in 2026 alone. Here are the details.
In a report released today, Counterpoint Research projects that advanced notebook display shipments will grow 11% in 2026 compared to 2025, with OLED notebook display shipments expected to rise 50% for the period.
The report notes that the 11% growth in advanced notebook display shipments will comfortably outperform the broader notebook display market, which Counterpoint says it expects to decline 3% year over year.
Counterpoint says it also expects OLED to widen its lead over MiniLED in the advanced notebook display market, rising from 56% of shipments in 2025 to 75% in 2026, while MiniLED is expected to drop from 44% to 25%.
The research company says part of the growth in OLED notebook panel shipments is expected to come from Apple’s adoption of the technology in its next-generation MacBook Pro, currently rumored to be announced on October 27 with a touchscreen display and a redesigned, significantly lighter chassis.
Here’s Counterpoint:
OLED notebook display shipments are forecast to rise 50% in 2026 and another 24% in 2027. Apple’s anticipated adoption of OLED in next-generation MacBook Pro models, alongside continued demand for premium AI PCs, is expected to drive this growth.
Another factor contributing to OLED’s growth, Counterpoint says, is technology and manufacturing advances such as tandem OLED architectures, which improve brightness, power efficiency, and panel longevity.
Apple already uses tandem OLED in the iPad Pro, where two OLED layers work together to deliver higher full-screen brightness. The company is widely expected to use a similar two-stack tandem OLED architecture for the upcoming 14-inch and 16-inch MacBook Pro models.
Looking ahead, Counterpoint expects OLED notebook panel shipments to grow at a compounded annual rate of 26% between 2024 and 2030, while panel revenue is forecast to grow at 35% per year over the same period.

Game PassGears Of War: E-DaySalesXbox 
Gears of War: E-Day is the best-rated game in the series in a long time. But excellence is no predictor of market success these days, especially for a platform with as many competing business models as Xbox. According to data firm Alinea Analytics, Microsoft’s newest console-exclusive shooter sold just 320,000 copies in its first week, but had 1.7 million players on Game Pass. So is that good or bad for The Coalition, one of many Xbox game teams currently worried about future cuts?
“Gears of War: E-Day has sold 230K copies,” Alinea’s Rhys Elliott posted on X on October 9, a week after the game’s early-access launch period began. “Steam accounts for 168K of those, with Xbox (console and PC) selling 62K. But thereâs more to this story, as Xbox makes up the bulk of revenue (almost three-quarters).”
He argues that Game Pass is cannibalizing much of the premium revenue fans might have spent on the game, without seemingly driving massive adoption from the rest of the subscriber-base, or some big uptick in the number of new sign-ups.
Gears of War: E-Day has sold 230K copies (@alineaanalytics estimates).
Steam accounts for 168K of those, with Xbox (console and PC) selling 62K. But thereâs more to this story, as Xbox makes up the bulk of revenue (almost three-quarters).
On Xbox, Game Pass is cannibalisingâ¦ pic.twitter.com/EH6GHFngYP
— Rhys Elliott (@superhys) October 9, 2026

According to Alinea’s estimates, none of which have been corroborated by Microsoft, there were 1.7 million players who accessed E-Day via a Game Pass subscription instead of buying it, 722,000 of whom paid $30 for the Premium Edition upgrade to access the game early. Meanwhile, the game is estimated to have sold 230,000 copies so far, 168,000 of which were on Steam. That comes to roughly $37 million in revenue so far, $26 million of which came from players on Xbox.
“In a world without day-one Game Pass, many of these folks would have bought E-Day at $70,” Elliott wrote. “They paid $30 for five days early access to something they donât own. Xbox is, in effect, charging its most valuable customers the least, based on a hangover from a tried-and-exhausted strategy from a previous administration.”
He notes that if the business model isn’t working for a game of the quality level that Gears of War: E-Day is at, then there clearly must be an issue with the business model. A cheap, ad-supported tier of Game Pass wouldn’t solve the problem of hardcore fans paying the least for access to a new exclusive, but increasing the price of day-one access might.
The logical conclusion, based on Elliot’s estimates and analysis, is that Microsoft will have to remove day-one first-party releases from the service, just as it announced it would do earlier this year with Call of Duty.

Kimberly Fenelon and her daughter were making snowmen in the front yard of her home in Hunts Point, the Bronx, when she was approached by a woman who handed her a stack of papers.
She had been served.
It was the day after Christmas, and a winter storm was dusting the New York City area with pristine white powder. As Fenelon realized what was happening, she burst into tears. Then so did her daughter, who was 6 at the time.
Fenelon, a single mother in her early 30s, had fallen behind on her mortgage. The home was once her mother’s, who died two years prior from cancer, leaving Fenelon to pay off the remaining balance.
The house was facing foreclosure.
“I was stunned,” Fenelon said. “I didn’t know what to do.”
Fenelon is one of more than 1,000 Bronx residents who were issued a foreclosure filing in 2025, according to an accounting from the Center for New York City Neighborhoods, an organization that supports local homeowners.
Foreclosure filings skyrocketed last year, according to a report from the real estate company Property Shark. The borough recorded 109 first-time foreclosures in the second quarter of this year, the highest in seven years, while foreclosures across the city declined.
Now, New York City, state, and local organizations are ramping up efforts to help people like Fenelon who have fallen behind on their mortgages, and are encouraging people to seek help before it reaches the point of foreclosure.
“ If you hit some sort of unforeseen bump or problem, there's help available,” said Christie Peale, the CEO of the Center for New York City Neighborhoods. “A lot of folks just don't know that.”
“ The earlier that you reach out for help, the easier it is for us to help you,” Peale added.
Community outreach efforts to get Peale’s message across are in full swing.
Last weekend, the New York attorney general’s office hosted a block-walking event through the North Bronx, where foreclosures are particularly concentrated.
Volunteers knocked on more than 1,100 doors to disseminate information about resources designed to help Bronx residents struggling with homeownership and to warn people about scams, like deed theft, another threat to homeownership in the borough.
“For many New Yorkers, home ownership represents a pinnacle of their success, of their career,” Attorney General Letitia James told a crowd of volunteers gearing up to knock on doors. The best way to protect homeowners, she added, is to equip them with resources.
“There cannot be prevention without education,” James said.
The event was followed by three separate clinics that offered free legal counsel for Bronx homeowners concerned about foreclosure. That includes the two standing clinics at the Bronx County civil courthouse, and a resource fair at Bronx House, a community center in Pelham Parkway, hosted by the Center for NYC Neighborhoods and the AG’s office. There, about 125 people sought assistance from city agencies and nonprofit organizations.
Peale said the efforts are responding to a more systemic challenge: the affordability crisis.
“ If your cost of living is increasing, if your property taxes are going up, if your income hasn't kept up with your cost of living, it's gonna be challenging,” Peale said. “ All of these things are putting pressure on people’s pocketbooks. And that can impact your ability to pay your mortgage on time.”
Other measures wait in the wings. Last month, Deputy City Council Speaker Nantasha Williams advanced a package of bills aimed at strengthening homeowner protections.
“Together, these efforts reflect a broader approach to protecting homeownership: strengthening our laws, investing in the services homeowners rely on, and making those resources easier to access,” Williams said in a statement.
The package of 10 bills includes an array of approaches to the issues homeowners face. One bill would require individuals to be enrolled in city-created property tax payment plans to help avoid missed payments.
Another would require financial literacy advisers to serve at older adult centers — housing counselors observe that many foreclosures come from a lack of estate planning.
A third bill would establish a public outreach and education program to inform struggling homeowners of the assistance available to them.
Joseph Rebella is a  supervising attorney of the Homeownership Protection Project at the Legal Aid Society in the Bronx. He helps run the weekly housing clinics at the Bronx County civil courthouse.
Not all of the bills help protect people from losing their homes, Rebella said.
Some, Rebella said, are “aimed at sort of discrete issues which are problems but that don't necessarily contribute to the volume of foreclosure or housing turnover.”
Nonetheless, he said, most seem like good ideas.
“ A lot of those bills involve connecting people to the services that already exist,” Rebella said. “There’s nothing negative to be said about that.”
Another proposal would add more than $500,000 to the Homeowner Stabilization Services Initiative, a newly established fund that supports organizations that provide legal and financial assistance to homeowners or those looking to buy. If passed, it would bring the budget for the program to about $5.69 million for the 2027 fiscal year.
Gladstone Johnson, the executive director of the Bronx Neighborhood Housing Services, a nonprofit organization that includes a foreclosure prevention program, said that increased funding would be “the major fix for us.”
“ We're not asking [the homeowners] for any money,” Johnson said. “We need more funding so that we can hire some more staff and reach out to more of these homeowners.”
The need for Johnson’s services has skyrocketed in recent years, he said. The Center for New York City Neighborhoods refers people to his organization for housing counseling.
In a typical year, Johnson said, he would usually receive 15 referrals to their foreclosure program. This year, the Center referred 60 people to him — more than four times the usual caseload — before the year even comes to a close.
The homeowner legal assistance clinics at the courthouse in the Bronx is a similar scene. The clinic is run out of a brightly lit room down a hallway with wood paneling, where yellow signs and arrows point toward the legal clinic.
Attorneys from the Legal Aid Society, New York Legal Assistance Group, and Legal Services NYC all help run the programming every Wednesday and Thursday from 10 a.m. to 4 p.m.
Anthony James came to the clinic this week for legal counsel after he said he received a letter in the mail about falling behind on his mounting debt. James was sitting on top of an $80,000 bill from Con Edison — he shared the invoice with Gothamist — on top of another nearly $80,000 still left on the mortgage of his four-dwelling house, which is worth over $1 million.
James told the attorney on duty, Martha de Jesus, the director of Neighborhood Stabilization Project at Bronx Legal Services, that there is no way he could make the $7,000 mortgage payments he owes the bank.
De Jesus asked detailed questions about his income, his expenses, and the tenants who are living in his dwellings. She pulled up his property records, having to hotspot herself on an iPad after giving up on the Wi-Fi in her office — “we’re not made of money here,” she said.
She gave James strict instructions to return next week with additional materials, including bank statements. She would help make him a budget and game plan that could help him save his home.
De Jesus said James’ case can be a success story — he is one of the few who came in before he was issued a mandatory court hearing. And while James’ debt is mounting, de Jesus explained that he still has more than $200,000 in equity on his home.
The challenge now, she said, will be to find a way for him to be able to afford his costly monthly payments. But, she said, “we have time to figure it out.”
As for Fenelon, she said her experience fighting for her home has been “incredibly humbling.”
But, after applying seven different times for the city’s “One Shot Deal,” the city’s emergency cash assistance program, she has finally been accepted, and her application is pending final approval.
Fenelon said the deal will hopefully provide her with a grant to pay off the remaining balance — she said $9,000 — on her home, and this nightmare will finally be over.
Now, she said she notices people all over the Bronx facing foreclosure.
“I don’t know if prices are going up, but [foreclosure] is everywhere,” Fenelon said.
Thankfully, she said, “I have the opportunity to keep my home. Some people just don’t have it.”

37,2026-10-09,"Three of the lenders using the agents invested in the round, and in Europe the same software would sit in the AI Act's high-risk class
Vesta has raised a $30M Series B to expand AI agents that handle mortgage origination work, including underwriting decisions, with three customer lenders among the investors. The same software sold in Europe would be high-risk under the AI Act, and GDPR already limits automated decisions of this kind.
Vesta has raised a $30M Series B to put AI agents inside mortgage lenders. Conversion Capital led the round, and three lenders already using the software invested alongside Citi Ventures and Andreessen Horowitz.
Chief executive Mike Yu said demand had “exploded in the last year” and revenue is up twelvefold, he told TechCrunch. Vesta has raised $85M in total and puts its market share below 5%.
Lenders on its platform originate more than $100B of mortgages a year, HousingWire reported. About 40% of tasks running through Vesta are now handled by agents and automated workflows.
Those tasks reach further than paperwork. Lenders use the agents for document review and data validation, and some for underwriting decisions, condition clearing and closing package review.
The appeal is cost. A US mortgage takes around 40 days to close and roughly $11,000 to produce, mostly labour. The Mortgage Bankers Association has put origination costs above $10,000 for years.
Yu credits one model. He named Anthropic’s Claude Sonnet 4.5 as the breakthrough, saying it followed configured instructions over the long stretches that mortgage work demands.
Automated lending decisions carry a record. The Markup found in 2021 that US lenders were 80% more likely to deny Black applicants than comparable white ones, after controlling for 17 financial factors.
Yu says responsibility does not move. Lenders remain accountable for underwriting whatever software they use, he said, and every action and the reasoning behind it is logged for audit.
Europe does not leave that to the vendor. The AI Act puts systems that evaluate the creditworthiness of natural persons in its high-risk class, and mortgage approval sits squarely inside it.
That brings obligations rather than guidance. Providers face conformity assessment and registration, while deployers must assign human oversight to competent staff, keep six months of logs and run a fundamental rights impact assessment.
Those duties were pushed back. Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 deferred them from August this year to 2 December 2027, enforced by an AI Office that can fine 3% of turnover.
One rule is not waiting. GDPR Article 22 already restricts decisions made solely by automated means where they carry legal or similarly significant effects, and a refused mortgage is the textbook case.
Europe already has a version of this. Taktile in Berlin raised $110M in June for agents that underwrite business loans, which point 5(b) does not reach because it covers natural persons.

38,2026-10-09,"Stellantis and Wayve aim for hands-free driving from 2028, each brand tuned to drive its own way. Going driverless means beating a careful human driver, Wayve’s CEO said.
Wayve chief executive Alex Kendall at Wave by Vento in Turin, 9 October 2026
Every car will one day need an AI driving system, just as it needs seatbelts and emergency braking, Wayve chief executive Alex Kendall said. He spoke with Stellantis chief executive Antonio Filosa at Wave by Vento in Turin on Friday. Morgan Stanley’s Adam Jonas moderated.
“I think over time we’re going to see this kind of technology be required in vehicles like a seatbelt, like an emergency braking system,” Kendall said.
The technology is affordable enough for mass-market cars, and what happens on roads today is unacceptable, he said.
“So it’s really morally wrong to not have some system, some intelligence in the vehicle to save those lives,” Kendall said.
Stellantis and Wayve announced their partnership in May. Stellantis is putting the Wayve AI Driver into its STLA AutoDrive platform for hands-free, supervised driving. The first launch is targeted for 2028 in North America, Stellantis said. At Wave, the companies gave demonstration rides in a Fiat 500e and a Maserati Grecale.
The idea to put the system in the Fiat 500 came from a handshake about five months ago, Kendall said. Wayve tunes the AI to drive “Italian style”. He said he drove the Maserati on a track in Turin the night before, in pouring rain.
Wayve wants systems that run on less than $1,000 of hardware so they can reach mass scale, he said. Wayve also has a deal to put its AI into Mercedes-Benz production cars. It runs a supervised robotaxi service in London with Uber.
Each Stellantis brand will drive differently, Filosa said. AI will make each vehicle a self-learning machine. A Jeep will learn differently from a Peugeot or a Maserati. Customers will feel the difference between a Maserati and a Jeep, Kendall said, though both are built for safety first.
The partnership works because the two sides complement each other, Filosa said. Wayve brings the AI. Stellantis brings brand, vehicle and safety knowledge, and the scale that brings costs down. Stellantis develops a product in 24 months today, and AI can shorten that, he said.
Kendall sees supervised driving as a stepping stone to eyes-off and driverless cars, not a separate business. Starting supervised introduces customers to the technology and builds the data needed to improve it, he said. It also gives a head start on supply chains and hardware, which take years in the car industry.
To go unsupervised, a system must be safer than a safety-conscious human driver, he said. That is a higher bar than an average driver. Wayve has training data from over 100 countries and has tested in 600 to 700 cities. It proves safety in simulation first, with its generative world model, Gaia, before it deploys.
Performance is growing by more than an order of magnitude a year, he said. The scaling curves look like those of language models. Kendall wants Wayve to be the intelligence layer for vehicles, and later for robotics too. Last month, he said Wayve’s AI would reach Mercedes cars within two years.

Jaguar kept us waiting two years, and what rolled out in New York on Tuesday looks suspiciously like a very expensive Braun radio on wheels. The tail is a wall of horizontal metal slats stretched across a windowless rear deck, and the nose wears a matching slatted box that could have been borrowed from a vintage hi-fi. I mean that fondly, sort of. The Type 01 starts at $130,500 in the US, with orders opening in early 2027 and deliveries in the second half. At that price, I would like the car to look like somebody finished it.
Carbon Noir’s Type 01 Sports Edition concept is the cure, and it works almost entirely by editing. The slatted front panel stays but now sits inside a black carbon surround that ties it to the headlights, so the nose reads as one band instead of a box stuck on a face. A lower stance, stepped overfenders, and open black wheels with gold calipers fix the proportions, and the blue-gray paint helps the carbon trim blend in. Out back, a red light bar carrying the JAGUAR wordmark, a finned diffuser, and a big pedestal wing give the tail something to do. Everything recognizable from Tuesday’s car is still there, just dressed for dinner.
Designer: Carbon Noir







Stretch a tape measure along the Type 01 and you get about 17 feet (5.2m) of car that stands only around 4.6 feet (1.4m) tall, according to Autocar. Almost all of that length is a single satin silver slab from sill to shoulder, interrupted by a charge flap and a pair of flush door handles. A strip of bronze sits low on the door, and it is the only thing out there that looks like a design decision. The car rolls on 23-inch wheels, yet the flat turbine-style covers read as gray discs from any distance. A wheel that size should look like jewelry, and these look like dinner plates.
Hagerty reports that the production car came first and the Type 00 concept was developed from it, so those slab sides are the design rather than something watered down on the way to the showroom. Jaguar has a rear three-quarter pressing it calls the Impossible Panel, where the aluminum is formed across five planes with three feature lines meeting at a single point. That is serious metalwork, and in a flat satin finish it is hard to appreciate. Panels that clever deserve some shadows to play with. A body kit happens to be a shadow delivery system.







Lowering the car and wrapping each tire in a stepped overfender does more work than any wing. The factory car has a visible gap between tire and arch, so the body seems to float above its wheels. Carbon Noir closes that gap, and the fenders flare out in layers that finally give the long flank a rhythm. Open black wheels with gold calipers replace the flat covers, and suddenly the wheels look as big as they are. The dark blue-gray paint pulls the carbon into the body, so the trim looks grown rather than glued on.
Tiered carbon canards stack up the lower bumper, and their edges pick up the same bronze tone as the strip on Jaguar’s door. That is a smart little callback, since it turns one stray detail on the stock car into a theme for the whole kit. A pair of sculpted vents also cut into the hood, which is dead flat on the factory car. Together they give the face a head-down, ready-to-pounce posture, which seems fair for something named after a big cat. The slats stay, the headlamps stay, and the Jaguar-ness survives the surgery.


The factory tail is one uninterrupted surface, with bright slats edge to edge, a black band below, and a plate floating in the middle. Carbon Noir splits it into zones. A red light bar with the JAGUAR wordmark crowns the slats, a finned diffuser anchors the bottom, and a pedestal wing perches on the deck like a tray on a butler’s arm. A wing that size would look silly on a small car, but a 17-foot GT has the deck space to carry it. Copper trim traces the diffuser edges too, which keeps the back tied to that door strip.
Carbon Noir is the designer who gave India’s auto rickshaw a Renault-style electric makeover a few weeks ago, so jumping from a three-wheeler to a $130,500 British GT is quite the commute. Both projects show an eye for the shape already hiding inside a design. Managing director Rawdon Glover told Robb Report that more than 50,000 people have registered interest and 78 percent are new to Jaguar. Newcomers spending six figures will probably notice what the stock car leaves unfinished. Orders open in early 2027, which leaves Jaguar plenty of time to scroll Instagram. Jaguar gave us the radio, and Carbon Noir added the subwoofer.

43,2026-10-09,"Alex Aminov, 37, doesn't know why he's on his current phone plan, or how much he's paying for it.
Recently, the Brooklyn-based software developer said he tried to cancel the landline included in his Optimum internet package, which cost an extra $25 a month. When he called to try to cancel it — the only way to get through — a representative convinced him that it would be cheaper to transfer the line to a mobile plan, and that he'd be eligible for a slew of discounts.
He said that representative told him they'd follow up to ensure that the transfer went through so the discounts could go into effect; she never called him back. When he got back in touch, a different representative said just some of those discounts would apply, and that they didn't know who he originally spoke with. A week or so later, a third representative called to ask why he wasn't using the number. He's still not quite sure what's happening with his various plans.
""Telecom providers or basically almost anything that has a monthly subscription, it's a nightmare to cancel it,"" Aminov said. ""In general, I would like to have an option to cancel anything online.""
An Optimum spokesperson said, ""We're committed to making it as easy as possible for customers to manage their services and get the support they need. Each customer has different needs.""
New York City is addressing this very struggle. As of October 1st, New Yorkers can file complaints against businesses that make canceling subscriptions unnecessarily difficult. The city's Department of Consumer and Worker Protection will enforce fines ranging from $525 for the first violation to up to $3,500 for repeat offenses, and businesses may be required to reimburse affected consumers for unwanted services.
The city's ""click to cancel"" rule requires companies to state their subscription terms clearly, explain people's rights when buying or canceling a membership, and provide a cancellation process as ""straightforward"" as the sign-up process.
A few hundred complaints were submitted in the first days of the rule, and around 6,000 New Yorkers had visited the click-to-cancel site, according to DCWP Commissioner Sam Levine. He said many complaints are about gyms, streaming services, and newer, smaller subscription services that consumers might find on Instagram, like a shaving club or meal prep subscription.
""I had one of these services, a meal prep service. I signed up for it because I think you got three free boxes. And then the boxes, they kept coming. I couldn't cook fast enough. I felt like Lucy and Ethel at the candy factory,"" Levine said. When he tried to cancel, he couldn't do it online. ""I got on the phone, they said, 'Are you sure? We can give you a discount. We want to send you more meals.' It's a huge pain.""
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
The roadblocks to successfully canceling a subscription disproportionately affect people who are older or not as tech-savvy, said Emily Peterson-Cassin, director of competition and market fairness at the non-profit Consumer Federation of America.
The rule addresses the ""annoyance economy,"" she said. ""We're tired of our time and annoyance being extracted in the same way as our money.""
Some companies are already changing their processes. Kari Saitowitz, chief marketing and creative officer of the New York Sports Club, said the company supports the new rule and adjusted its cancellation process the day it took effect.
""The easier you make it for somebody to cancel, we believe the easier you make it for them to sign up,"" she said. ""It's good for the business, and it's good for the consumer.""
NYSC's parent company was previously sued by the New York State Attorney General for allegedly charging illegal fees and prohibiting membership cancellation, ultimately leading to a settlement and bankruptcy.
Saitowitz said the company's new leadership has made an effort to increase transparency and ease the process. NYSC made online membership cancellation available in February 2025, when a state law requiring the option took effect. As of October 1st, all additional recurring services and amenities at the gym, such as access to personal training and lockers, can also be canceled online. Previously, members had to either go to the club in person or contact it by phone or email to cancel these subscriptions.
Veronica Yurovsky, a 39-year-old software engineer in Brooklyn, has already submitted a complaint to the DCWP. She tried canceling her Spectrum Internet service several times, and when she failed again just days after ""click to cancel"" took effect, she filed a complaint saying that there seemed to be no way to end service online.
""It feels more effective than screaming into a pillow, I suppose,"" Yurovsky said.
Asked about Yurovsky's experience to the contrary, which was reviewed by Business Insider, Don Kaplan, senior director of communications at the company, said, ""Spectrum complies with all applicable laws and regulations, including New York City's requirements regarding subscription cancellations.""
The goal is for consumers to hear back from his agency within weeks, if not days, Levine said. ""If people want to trust government to do the big things, they need to be able to trust the government can take on the everyday things that drive us crazy,"" he said.
Yurovsky would love to live in a world where firms comply with the new policy. If anything, it might make her more inclined to try out a service knowing that she wouldn't have to risk becoming ensnared in a seemingly infinite billing cycle.
""It takes a load off to know that people or companies are holding up their end of the bargain,"" she said.

44,2026-10-09,"Brant Eckert thought he couldn't make his fresh-out-of-college $60,000 salary work in New York City, so he moved to San Antonio. It's a move that other Zoomers are making, too.
Texas has been a beacon for those looking for an affordable lifestyle for years, but the latest bunch of Lone-Star movers aren't quite like the last.
Redfin Chief Economist Daryl Fairweather told Business Insider that the pandemic movers of the early 2020s were bringing their coastal salaries with them due to the rise in remote work and were looking to stretch their dollars. However, more recently, Texas has been luring younger movers looking for early-career opportunities.
""They're probably not choosing based on remote work, because those entry-level jobs tend to be more in-person, or at least hybrid,"" Fairweather said. ""They're not thinking about 'Where can I take my salary and where it will go the furthest?' They're thinking, 'Where do I want to start my career?'""
Redfin analyzed US Census data and found that millennial and Gen Z movers are choosing Texas cities more than anywhere else. San Antonio, for example, had the highest net migration among Gen Z movers, at 10,678 residents. From 2014 to 2024, Texas cities claimed three of the top five spots, with Austin and Dallas both posting gains in the thousands.
In a separate report, Redfin and Glassdoor ranked cities for new grads, with Dallas, Houston, and Austin included in the top 10.
Dallas and Houston had the highest net migration counts by far among millennial movers, with 13,072 and 16,365, respectively.Millennials, a little further along in life than Zoomers, are on the hunt for more space — something Texas has a lot of at an affordable price.
Fairweather said that while coastal cities like New York City or Los Angeles — which both had net migration losses in the tens of thousands among Gen Z and millennials — often offer higher salaries, Texas cities offer a more diverse job market and the opportunity to live comfortably for less.
""I think especially for people who are not going for that $300,000 salary — they're just trying to build a middle-class life in a place that's affordable to be middle class — Texas offers them that,"" she said.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Native Californians Gerardo and Tomi Ruiz traded San Bernardino for San Antonio in 2024 after a fire damaged their home. They discussed starting over somewhere else in California, but landed on seeking a more affordable life in Texas.
""Our first impression of Texas was how cheap the rent is. We paid about $2,350 in San Bernardino for a three-bedroom, one-bathroom apartment,"" Gerardo told Business Insider in 2024.""We're now in a two-bedroom, two-bathroom place in San Antonio and pay $1,250.""
Tomi told Business Insider at the time that as a phlebotomist in California, she was making about $25 an hour, but in Texas, it was closer to $16 to $20 an hour. The savings elsewhere made up for it, she said.
Pandemic darling Denver received an influx of movers in 2020 and 2021, but has recently experienced a net loss of Gen Z and millennial residents. Fairweather said that Colorado and Texas aren't all that different, but while Colorado becomes more expensive, Texas remains the more affordable option.
""It really does come down to housing affordability and not having to really scrape by with roommates or worry about whether you're able to afford your rent,"" said Fairweather. ""I think in Texas, it's just smoother sailing on that front.""

45,2026-10-09,"It's hard to say what Joanna Gaines is more famous for at this point: her home designs or her food.
Gaines and her husband became household names through ""Fixer Upper,"" but over a decade later, they're full-blown moguls who are constantly expanding their Magnolia empire. For Gaines, that has meant leaning into new food ventures, from the Magnolia Table restaurant in Waco to her ever-growing list of cookbooks.
And last year, she entered the frozen food game. In October 2025, Gaines launched a line of frozen baked goods through her Magnolia Table brand.
I decided to try every item in the line so I could rank them. Here's what I thought.
Magnolia Table's line of baked goods was first sold exclusively at Target, like the Hearth & Hand with Magnolia brand. Now, shoppers can also buy the line at HEB and Central Market.
All of the baked goods in the collection were inspired by food Joanna Gaines makes at home, or that's served at Silos Baking Co. or Magnolia Table in Waco, Texas.
The line originally featured six items:
And Gaines later added two more products in recent months:
As of October 2026, each item costs $10.99, except for the biscuits, which are $9.99.
I'm no stranger to Gaines' food offerings, having dined at Magnolia Table in Waco and made her homemade biscuit recipe. When I saw the news of Magnolia Table's frozen line, I knew I had to try it.
After grabbing the collection through a pickup order at Target, I spent an afternoon baking the original six items, and a year later, I grabbed the Brownie Cookies and Prize Pig Biscuits after they were released to review them as well. Here's my ranking from worst to best.
When I first glanced at the box of the After School Banana Bread, I saw ""ready to enjoy in minutes"" written on the front. However, about an hour into my baking endeavors, I looked more closely and realized the bread required more preparation than I had thought.
The directions said to thaw the bread in its bakeable tray for three to four hours before baking it at 350 degrees Fahrenheit for 30 minutes. There was an option to microwave the bread from frozen, but I wanted to bake it for the best possible result.
Thawing the banana bread wasn't a huge deal, but remembering to take it out of the freezer hours before baking didn't seem particularly convenient.
When I asked the brand about the timing, Magnolia Table said, ""We know so many families love our After School Banana Bread recipe, and we wanted to give them a way to recreate that same warm, cozy aroma at home by baking it in the oven.""
""At the same time, we know life gets busy, so we also included a quicker option for those moments when convenience matters most,"" the brand added. ""If you're warming from frozen, you can simply place the loaf in the microwave and enjoy it in just a few minutes. Both methods are there to make sure families can experience that homemade comfort, whether it's a weekend bake or a weekday treat.""
I'm not generally a huge banana bread fan, but the Magnolia Table iteration was fairly tasty.
It wasn't too banana-forward and had a comforting taste when I took a bite fresh from the oven. The bread offered easy eating, and as its name indicates, it seems like a good option for an afternoon snack for kids.
The flavor didn't really stand out, though, especially compared to other items in the line, and I don't think it was worth the hours of thawing. I would probably make it in the microwave next time, though I'm unsure how that would change the texture.
I've always loved a good cinnamon roll, and although homemade rolls are particularly good, I have no problem with frozen options. I spent most Saturdays of my childhood begging my mom to make the Pillsbury or Sister Schubert brand of refrigerated rolls.
As a frozen cinnamon roll connoisseur, I was excited to try Gaines' take on the classic pastry.
In Magnolia Table's pack, four rolls come in a bakeable tray sealed inside plastic wrap. I was a bit surprised by the small number of cinnamon rolls, but when I asked Magnolia Table about it, it said ""a great deal of consideration"" went into deciding how many of each baked good to include in its packs.
""Some decisions were guided by simple kitchen logic — like including a dozen cookies, which naturally feels right when baking at home,"" Magnolia Table said. ""We also relied on research and consumer insights to ensure each product offers both exceptional quality and convenience. Ultimately, every package was designed to feel intentional and familiar, much like something made in your own kitchen.""
The directions on the box instruct you to remove the rolls from the plastic wrap and then let them rest for 45 minutes to an hour before baking at 350 degrees Fahrenheit for 23 to 25 minutes. The resting wasn't my favorite, but I liked that you could throw the rolls into the oven in the tray.
A packet of icing was also included, but the directions didn't specify what to do with it while the rolls rested. I left my packet on my kitchen counter.
My cinnamon rolls were ready in about 25 minutes, with the edges slightly crispy and the center gooey.
I poured my icing — which had softened from sitting on the counter — over the cinnamon rolls, and it spread out fairly thin. The icing might have had a better consistency if I had kept it in the refrigerator while the rolls thawed, but the directions didn't indicate that I should have done so.
I informed Magnolia Table about my icing consistency issues. The brand expressed appreciation for the feedback and clarified that the icing could be left out without compromising safety.
""Our icing is completely safe to leave out while the rolls thaw and bake, but for those who prefer a thicker consistency, placing it in the fridge is a great option,"" Magnolia Table said.
Despite my issues with the icing, the cinnamon rolls smelled inviting, so I was optimistic when I took a bite. It wasn't bad, but it reminded me of cinnamon rolls I've had at the mall. The cinnamon lining the swirl had a slightly artificial taste, and it was almost cloyingly sweet.
I would prefer to eat other frozen or refrigerated cinnamon roll brands over Magnolia Table's, as I think they taste better and offer more bang for your buck.
I absolutely recommend Gaines' homemade cinnamon roll recipe, though.
The Prize Pig Biscuits were an addition that Gaines made to her frozen collection in 2026. Unlike her Buttermilk Biscuits, the new iteration is packed with bacon, cheddar, and chive for a more savory taste.
The Prize Pig Biscuits come in a pack of six, and the instructions say to bake them at 400 degrees for 18 to 20 minutes.
As with most of the collection, my biscuits took a bit longer to bake than the instructions said they would. I pulled them out of the oven after 22 minutes and let them rest before tasting.
I found it challenging to get an even bake on the biscuits, as the bottoms cooked faster than the tops. When I pulled my biscuits out, the bottoms were slightly overbaked, while the tops were a little softer than I prefer for a biscuit texture.
Still, they smelled good, and I was pleased with the taste when I took a bite. The bacon, chive, and cheddar blended well together, creating a savory flavor that wasn't over the top.
The textural experience was strange when they were hot from the oven, though. The bottom was overcooked, while the interior and top were moister than I prefer for a biscuit, which I expect to be flaky and soft. They felt more cohesive when I let them cool longer, as the tops got a little flakier, but I prefer to eat my biscuits hot, which leaves me at an impasse.
Considering they're baked from frozen, I think the Prize Pig Biscuits are pretty good, but they don't hold a candle to the Buttermilk Biscuits (more on that in a minute).
The three flavors of cookies released in the original Magnolia Table line came in rectangular boxes with a set of twelve plastic-wrapped cookies inside. They also had the same directions: Bake the cookies 2 inches apart on a parchment-lined baking sheet at 350 degrees on the middle rack for 15 to 18 minutes.
I was able to bake the chocolate chip and Silo cookies at the same time, though one had to go on the bottom rack. I switched them halfway through to ensure both were fully baked.
In my oven, the cookies needed to bake for 20 minutes until they were cooked through with a soft center.
Of the three, Magnolia Table's Silo Cookies ranked lowest for me, but they were still really good. They blended the best parts of an oatmeal cookie and a chocolate chip cookie by including peanut butter, oatmeal, chocolate, and walnuts.
At first bite, I found the Silo Cookie to be mostly peanut butter-forward. I liked that they weren't too cloying, and when I got a bite with chocolate, the flavor became more inviting.
All three sets of cookies were also moist and tasted fresh despite being frozen, which was a relief.
Gaines' chocolate chip cookies were quite tasty.
The cookie had a salty-sweet taste, and I liked that the chocolate chips were sizable and generously included in each cookie, ensuring there was chocolatey goodness in every bite.
They didn't taste particularly distinct from other frozen chocolate chip cookies I've had, but they were yummy.
Of the original three cookies, I was most skeptical of the Classic Sugar Cookies, as I often find that frozen sugar cookies can be overly sweet.
However, Gaines' cookies were the perfect balance of sugary and buttery. When I took a bite of one fresh from the oven, it melted in my mouth.
The sugar cookies ended up being my favorite of the original three, which was a shock. I could easily see myself buying them again.
Gaines added the Brownie Cookies to her collection later, but I was pleased to discover they had the same baking instructions as the other cookies.
I moved between my Magnolia Table baking sessions, so I was using a different oven while preparing the Brownie Cookies. As with the other cookies, though, they took longer than the allotted 16-18 minutes to bake. It was also a bit tricky to tell when they were done since the dough was so dark. I ended up baking the Brownie Cookies for about 22 minutes, which left them chewy.
They were absolutely worth the wait, though. The Brownie Cookies were exactly as promised: a mix between a crumbly cookie and a fudgy brownie. The gooey center was heavenly, and I loved the level of chocolate they offered. If I wanted the taste of a brownie without actually making one, I'd definitely grab the Brownie Cookies again.
Most fans of Gaines are likely familiar with her biscuits, whether they have tried her homemade recipe themselves or have had them at the Magnolia Table restaurant in Waco.
Her biscuits are known for being deliciously crumbly and buttery, and I was skeptical that the frozen version could live up to the homemade one.
Six biscuits come in a pack, and they bake at 400 degrees Fahrenheit for 20 minutes. The directions also say to top the biscuits with melted butter and salt right out of the oven.
I tried a biscuit warm from the oven after I topped it with butter and salt.
It was buttery and crumbly, and I was pleased it wasn't dry. The salt balanced out the rich butter, creating an addictively delicious bite. I instantly thought of several meals my husband and I eat regularly that I would love to pair with the biscuits.
I don't think they would be best for feeding a crowd, since there are only six in a pack, and I still prefer the homemade version. However, frozen biscuits are a great choice for a family weeknight meal.
Overall, I think Magnolia Table's collection is a fun venture for the Gaineses. It's a playful way for their fans to easily access some of the foods they offer in Waco, and the food is tasty overall.
I could see myself buying the Buttermilk Biscuits and all the cookies again, but I don't think I'd revisit the savory biscuits, cinnamon rolls, or banana bread. The banana bread and cinnamon rolls were a bit too time-consuming to make, and, in the case of the latter, there are already better alternatives on the market. And while the Prize Pig Biscuits are good, I would rather eat the original Buttermilk Biscuits with savory sides for the best biscuit experience possible.
Considering Gaines has published three cookbooks, I'm excited to see what other frozen foods she creates next.
