46,2026-10-09,"
					By Liz Shackleton 
pmc-media-editor-2024

	Asian Contents & Film Market (ACFM), the industry platform of Busan International Film Festival, launched three new programs last year, which are starting to yield results as they move into their second edition.

	Among these, InnoAsia hosts a range of panels, workshops and other events focusing on advanced technologies and innovative business strategies, including AI, Virtual Production, microdrama, VR & AR and branded content. As ACFM market director Ellen YD Kim explains below, projects introduced through InnoAsia’s AI works-in-progress showcase last year have already gone on to raise finance and reach the international market.

	











	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					Vietnam's V Studios Seals Multiple Deals On 'Red Woman' Including Sales To North America & Japan		
			















	News
	



	
		
					International Insider: 'Doctor Who' Regeneration Underway; MIPCOM Preview; Busan Takeaways		
			







	Companies attending this segment of the market include Google DeepMind, Amazon Web Services (AWS), Kling AI and Alibaba Group. Kling AI, the AI video arm of China’s Kuaishou, is hosting a panel on the second day of the market, New Cinematic Possibilities: Reimagining Filmmaking, with speakers including Korean director Kim Sungsoo (12.12: The Day) and Lee Sangwook, a producer at MBC C&I AI Contents Lab.




			Watch on Deadline		






	In addition, ACFM is hosting the second edition of DocSquare, which brings together Asian documentary filmmakers, distributors, festivals and funders. This year, the market is hosting an ‘Impact Asia Impact Day’, during which a study of documentaries in Korea, Japan and Taiwan will be released.

	Another initiative entering its second year is ‘The A’, a conference summit and in-depth report covering 15 Asian territories. This year’s panels include a session on Asian film distribution, with speakers from companies including Korea’s Noori Pictures, Thailand’s Chada Entertainment and Mongolia’s Filmbridge; and an investor panel, featuring Solaire Partners, Pathe Films and Hive Media Corp, among other speakers.

	All the market’s other long-running programs are also returning – Asian Project Market (APM), which this year has selected 30 projects from 20 countries; Busan Story Market, which is presenting 25 pieces of IP from Korea, Japan, Taiwan and Indonesia; Asian Cinema Fund; Producers Hub, and Platform Busan.

	










	Two Asian film industries will be under the spotlight at this year’s ACFM – Thailand has been anointed the first ever ‘Country of Honor’, while Japan is the ‘Focus Country’ in the Producers Hub.

	Speaking to Deadline in the run-up to ACFM, Kim explains that the market aims to help the industry navigate one of its most turbulent periods – a time of mergers, shrinking capital, restructuring of platforms and the disruptions of microdrama and AI – by providing a platform to “Connect, Strengthen, and Expand”.

	ACFM is taking place at BEXCO from October 10-13, during Busan International Film Festival (October 6-15).

DEADLINE: How was InnoAsia received by the Asian industry last year and what results have you seen so far?

ELLEN YD KIM: InnoAsia has established itself as the place in Asia to see where generative AI filmmaking actually stands. The industry values it for bringing together in one place the AI production tools and pipelines that global tech companies and creators are working with today.

	AI projects introduced at last year’s Work-in-Progress Showcase have gone on to raise production financing and participate in the Cannes Marché du Film. Mateo AI Studio signed a promotional partnership with Kling AI; was presented at Kling AI’s session in Cannes; and raised $1.5m (KRW2bn) in pre-Series A funding. The company’s fully AI-generated feature Raphael is now being readied for release.

	Producer Ham Seul-gi and director Seol Woo-in, who took part last year with an AI short, founded AIKNOW Studio and completed an AI feature, Steelay, which they will preview at this year’s WIP before it opens in Korean theaters on October 20.

	Inshorts, an AI upscaling company, went from InnoAsia’s start-up strand to being presented as Korea’s representative at EFM Startups in Berlin and at Cannes Next, and has since been expanding its business with contracts in Japan, China and other Asian markets. The company returns to InnoAsia this year as an exhibitor.

DEADLINE: How are ACFM exhibitor numbers tracking this year compared to last year? Any new participants?

KIM: This year 339 institutions and companies from 32 countries and territories are exhibiting, including four with online-only booths. That is up 17% from 289 exhibitors from 31 countries last year, and the fourth consecutive year of growth, from 264 in 2023 and 275 in 2024.

	The strongest interest has come from Thailand, this year’s Country of Honor, which has grown from 17 exhibitors to 28. Japan, the Producer Hub Focus Country, is now the largest international presence with 46 exhibitors. Growth across Southeast Asia is also clear: the Philippines is up from 15 to 21 exhibitors, and Cambodia from one to nine.

	Europe’s presence is also notable. The UK has grown from one exhibitor last year to seven this year, with companies such as Protagonist Pictures and Cornerstone Films under the UK Film stand. Leading European sales companies including Goodfellas and Le Pacte from France, Pluto Film from Germany and Heretic from Greece. And more countries are joining for the first time or returning, among them the Czech Republic and Slovakia, Bangladesh and Kazakhstan.

	The mix has changed too. Tech exhibitors have increased from seven to 18, and national agencies and film commissions from 62 to 79. The market is broadening from trading finished films into a place where technology, co-production, location attraction and the promotion of national industries feed into one another.

	










DEADLINE: Why did ACFM decide to spotlight Thailand and Japan this year? What strengths do these markets currently have? And what can other markets learn from them?

KIM: The two countries are taking part at different levels. Thailand is the ‘Country of Honor’ of the market as a whole. Launched this year, Country of Honor is the highest tier of ACFM’s country partnerships, and it presents a country’s industry as a whole across a wide range of ACFM’s programs. Japan is the Focus Country of Producer Hub, which works on a different scale. It concentrates on producers, and through them on the driving force of production: their projects, their co-production experience and the environment they work in.

	Thailand has been developing content as a key pillar of its national soft power strategy. In 2025, the country hosted more than 540 foreign productions, and it offers eligible foreign productions cash rebates of up to 30%. At the same time, Thai titles have entered the Netflix Global Top 10, proving the competitiveness of its own content. Its strength is that it is both an international production base and a market with competitive local content.

	This year Thailand presents five features as the inaugural line-up of the Busan Story Market’s new ‘Remake IP’ section; co-hosts our opening reception, and is bringing 28 exhibitors. The ties with Busan also run deep: from Pen-ek Ratanaruang’s Ploy in 2006 to last year’s APM selection, Thai projects have been traveling to the world through Busan for 20 years.

	Japan today is Asia’s most dynamic co-production partner. On top of its longstanding strength in independent and arthouse cinema, bold policies centered on grants and incentives are expanding international collaboration into commercial films and series. This year ten Japanese producers will pitch their projects in Busan, including co-productions with Korea, Canada, Indonesia and France. Japan’s Agency for Cultural Affairs (Bunka-cho) is taking part directly for the first time.

	Our focus on these two countries is the result of two governments’ determination to take their content industries overseas and meeting the partnership efforts ACFM has been steadily making. Thailand needed a market that could turn its soft power strategy into real business, and ACFM proposed the Country of Honor program. Japan, backed by new support policies, was looking for a place to meet international partners, and came to us first to say it wanted to take part as the Producer Hub Focus Country. The lesson for other markets is that a government’s commitment produces results when it meets a concrete platform ready to put it to work.

DEADLINE: The Korean industry appears to have picked up this year – at least with some big box office hits and titles selected for major festivals. What are your thoughts on the current health of the industry?

KIM: The recovery is real. Box office for the first half of this year reached $431m (KRW 579bn), up 42% on the same period last year, while admissions rose 34% to 57.05 million. Korean films led that recovery taking 64% of first-half revenue, with The King’s Warden becoming the highest-grossing film in Korean box office history.

	What interests me more is the breadth. Success was not limited to period dramas and action tentpoles. Mid-sized films such as the romance Once We Were Us and horror film Salmokji: Whispering Water also succeeded, and audiences in their twenties were at the heart of it. A film’s strength and word of mouth, not its release date, are now deciding the box office. We have been reminded that audiences never left the cinema.

	










	I also do not think the health of Korean cinema should be measured by the domestic box office alone. Korean IP and production know-how are at work inside local hits across Asia. But it’s too early to say the industry has regained its health. The King’s Warden alone accounted for 28% of first-half revenue, and a single distributor took close to half the market.

	While the theatrical market grew, the number of Korean releases fell by 22. The average return on Korean commercial films in 2025 was minus 33%, the lowest since the pandemic, and in June one of the major cinema chains filed for corporate rehabilitation. A recovery that leans on one hit is not unique to Korea either: this year’s The A Report shows the same pattern in India, China and Taiwan.

	So I see this as a rebound, not yet a structural recovery. Audience demand has been confirmed, and so has the strength of Korean IP. The task now is to rebuild a production base that can steadily supply films of varied scale to meet that demand.

DEADLINE: Has the Korean government introduced any new policies to support the industry since ACFM took place last year?

KIM: There has clearly been policy change over the past year – in three directions. First, production support has grown sharply. The Mid-Budget Film Production Support Program, introduced last year, supported nine films with $7.5m (KRW10bn) in its first year. This year the main budget of about $15m (KRW20bn) was joined by a supplementary budget of about $19m (KRW25.8bn), supporting 32 films in total.

	In a single year the budget has more than quadrupled and the number of films has more than tripled. The cap on support per film rose from $1.2m (KRW1.5bn) to $1.9m (KRW2.5bn). The selections include new films by Byun Young-joo, Lee Joon-ik and Um Tae-hwa. Development funding rose by more than 80% and the government’s contribution to the film account of the Fund of Funds increased to $36.5m (KRW49bn). Public money is filling the space that private investment has left.

	Second, international co-production and technology have become policy priorities. A new international co-production category within the mid-budget program selected Bound, by Australian director Eddy Bell, which on completion will be the first Korea-Australia co-production. The supplementary-budget selections include a new film by Japanese director Koji Fukada, which is also a recipient of this year’s Co-Production Support Fund from ACFM’s Asian Cinema Fund.

	The Korean Film Council (KOFIC) has separately launched a pilot program for co-productions and Korea and Canada signed an audiovisual co-production treaty this year. The AI-related budget has tripled and a new AI Film Production Support Program has been introduced.

	Third, this direction continues into next year. In the government’s 2027 budget proposal, announced in September, film support is set at $128.6m (KRW172.6bn), about 30% more than this year. Support for international co-productions would rise from six films to 14, and a new program would support foreign productions shooting in Korea.

	There are also issues still under debate. On the issue of holdbacks, the window between theatrical release and online release, the government and the industry had aimed to reach a voluntary agreement by August, but did not. Discussion also continues on bringing streaming platforms into the legal framework for the audiovisual industry, and on requiring them to contribute to the film fund.

	










	That said, a bigger budget does not by itself solve the problem. Policy funding also grew last year, yet actual investment was slow to follow. Investors say there are too few projects worth investing in, while producers say they cannot find money. Closing that gap is the next task, and it is the reason we created the Investor Summit within The A Summit this year.

47,2026-10-09,"The stock market has been able to shrug off surging interest rates thanks to strong corporate earnings, but its margin for error is shrinking, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.
""When we started this cycle, the expected returns of equities were much higher than the expected returns of bonds,"" the Bridgewater Associates founder told CNBC on Thursday.
""Because of that change in pricing, that cushion has come down,"" he added.
The benchmark 10-year Treasury yield hit 5.36% this week, its highest level since 2002, as investors grappled with persistent inflation, heavy government borrowing, and expectations that the Federal Reserve will keep interest rates higher for longer.
Yet US stocks have remained resilient. The S&P 500 is up more than 13% this year despite pulling back from a recent record high, with enthusiasm over artificial intelligence and robust corporate earnings powering much of the rally.Higher bond yields make fixed-income investments more attractive relative to equities, leaving stocks with less room to absorb further increases in borrowing costs.
However, strong earnings growth has been enough to offset the drag from higher interest rates for now.""We're in the part of the cycle where interest rates can rise without sending the equity market down because there's enough earnings growth and there's enough expected return,"" Dalio said. 
""But when that cushion comes down, then you're coming later into that cycle.""
The AI buildout is adding to the competition for capital. The enormous capital required to build AI infrastructure is increasing competition for investors' money at the same time that governments are issuing large amounts of debt, adding to upward pressure on financing costs.
Despite the growing demand for capital, Dalio said financial conditions have not yet tightened enough to meaningfully constrain access to capital.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
He pointed to relatively narrow corporate credit spreads — the extra yield investors demand to hold corporate debt over US Treasurys — as evidence that investors are still willing to finance companies at relatively low premiums.
""We know that we haven't yet put the brakes on,"" Dalio said, adding that investors were only beginning to become more selective about where they deploy capital.

48,2026-10-09,"It was getting late, and the movers still hadn't arrived with the beds or the crib, so I tucked my three kids (5, 2, and 11 months) into bed on an air mattress on the floor of an otherwise empty bedroom.
I wanted to tell the kids that, once everything was set up, their room wouldn't look so bland. But the truth was, even with all of our belongings moved in, we'd only have the essentials. 
I'd sold a lot of furniture and decor ahead of the move (both to get some extra cash and make the moving process less strenuous). Plus, my ex was keeping some of our shared possessions. When all was said and done, I was down to the basics.
After the kids fell asleep, I stood in my new empty living room. I realized this: At 34, I was starting over.
My husband and I met when we were 17 and were together for the next 17 years. We always used to say we were lucky to have met so early and grown up together. We thought we'd be together forever.
But somewhere along the way, things changed. It wasn't that we argued all the time or didn't love each other. It was just that we'd become different people. The 17-year-old versions of ourselves were a perfect match. But I guess the 34-year-old versions weren't. It seemed smarter to just focus on being good parents and great friends.
But there was still so much to figure out. I had to decide how I was going to transition from stay-at-home mom to breadwinner. I needed to learn how to manage all household to-do lists on my own. Plus, I wanted to focus on the kids and help them through this massive change.
It was daunting and emotional. But I could take it day by day. My first task was figuring out a landing zone.
I could've stayed in the townhome my ex and I bought four years before, but I wanted to start fresh in a new place where I could make new memories. A blank slate.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
So we put the place on the market, and I moved closer to my mom, where I could lean on her as both a babysitter and a shoulder to cry on. I focused on my first big project: making my new home feel like a warm, nurturing place for my kids and me.
We had what we needed: beds, a couch, a small dining table from one friend, and a living room rug from another. I got my blank slate — but maybe it was too blank.
We had a lot of clothes with no drawers to put them in, books with no bookshelves, and a lot of empty walls.
The walls bothered me the most. Our old place was covered in wedding pictures and smiling family photos, and while I'll admit they made me sad as my marriage was unraveling, they were still reminders of happy times. At the very least, they brought color to the rooms.
Meanwhile, these new, empty walls felt sterile and a little intimidating.
So I started hanging things up.
My daughter's preschool art pieces skipped the fridge and were lovingly affixed above the couch with painter's tape. I went to thrift stores and sifted through all the frames and paintings, finding some gems for around $10 each. I also collected mirrors to make the rooms look bigger and brighter.
Then my mom offered up a box of old art pieces I'd done in elementary school and high school, and even some oils I'd made in a college class.
I'll admit: they aren't masterpieces. But they were both free and cute. I was glad to display the watercolor of a cat I made when I was 10, among others. Why not? I wasn't trying to impress anyone with a magazine-ready ""grown-up house."" I just wanted it to feel homey. Plus, it was nice to connect with a younger, pre-relationship version of me. It was nice to remember who she was.
Pictures of the kids were also easy wall decorations, but I swapped framed snaps of me and my ex with a silly picture of me with Pooh Bear at Disneyland and a treasured shot of me meeting Hollywood icon Dick Van Dyke. ""Look,"" I told a friend when she came to visit, ""it's the men in my life.""
Furniture was the next project.
With the walls covered, I went online for some secondhand furniture. I was tired of sitting on the laminate floor whenever I played with the kids, so I searched mainly for chairs and rugs.
I thought this would be an easy task, but after browsing listings on Facebook Marketplace, I was confused. I didn't know what I wanted.
The problem was that my husband had such a great sense of style, so I always let him pick our furniture and decor. And it worked out: I loved his clean, midcentury vibe. But was it what I'd pick if left to my own devices? I wasn't sure.
At first, I tried to search for the clean lines and angles I was used to, but after a few days of searching, I found more whimsical stuff caught my eye. I ended up driving 20 minutes to pick up a pink velvety chair. There was a green bookshelf I simply had to have. It was stuff I never would've looked at before the move, but now, I was leaning into more girly, softer styles.
I used my cat watercolor as my guide. Whenever I looked at it, with its earthy tones and cozy, soft vibe, I felt a warm sense of pride and familiarity. Maybe this was my style. Or at least a small version of it.
Now, it's only been eight months since we moved, since the reset, and the place is finally starting to feel like home. There are pictures on the wall, furniture I love, and a lot of noise and activity. My kids seem comfortable and happy. And I do too. There's something great about starting over, starting from nearly scratch, and ending up with something good.
But of course, the place isn't perfect. Some corners still feel sparse, and I've got some fixes and changes on my to-do list. It's a work in progress; I think it might be for years. And maybe right now I'm a work in progress, too. That's OK.
Recently, my mom came over after a long weekend of hanging pictures and arranging furniture. ""Wow, you're really moved in now!"" she said. ""It looks good.""
I thanked her but said I wanted to hang some floating shelves and swap some paintings around. She interrupted and wrapped an arm around me.
""Really, you've done a good job,"" she said. ""I love it.""
I looked around, feeling proud, ""I do too.""

PC sales are continuing to fall

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Worldwide PC shipments fell a staggering 20% year-over-year during the third quarter of 2026, per the latest IDC analysis, meaning 15.8 million fewer PCs were shipped compared with the same period in 2025.
The findings broadly align with Omdia's findings, which revealed a 21% drop, while IDC's data even shows a worrying 9% quarter-on-quarter decrease.
While the figures have a small degree of variability, both agencies agree on one thing – the AI boom is responsible for the PC industry's problems, and it all comes down to the price of chips and other components.
""The impact of upstream component cost increases has now fully worked its way through to end-market pricing,"" Omdia Research Director Ishan Dutt explained. DRAM and SSDs are the main offenders, Omdia explained, noting that their share of a total PC's price has risen from around 15% to 40%.
Omdia also anticipates DRAM revenue globally to top $372 billion this year, up from $151 billion last year, as AI data center-driven growth shows no sign of stopping.
But while it ultimately means the PC market could experience long-term price rises, IDC dropped one key piece of information that could make now the perfect time to buy a new PC.
Because so many OEMs have stockpiled in a bid to offset some of those rising costs, they're now likely worried about shifting so much stock. ""Channels are concerned about high inventory and low demand, so promotions are possible,"" IDC explained, noting that while prices are elevated compared with this time last year, there could be some momentary relief before yet more rises.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
""With macro conditions worsening, the risk is that the outlook for the next few quarters gets worse before it gets better,"" consumer devices Research Director Jitesh Ubrani explained.

50,2026-10-09,"In the spring, we hit a pretty wild milestone: Oxide paid income tax.  And
not because of some unusual transaction or one-time event, but because
our ordinary operations (selling computers!) generated taxable income
after accounting for the costs of components, manufacturing, salaries,
and running the rest of the business.
Of course, we each individually pay income tax
every year; is it that surprising that a company is doing what we all do every
spring?
Well, yes, it is:  most startups donât pay income tax because most startups
arenât profitable!  Indeed, startups seek investment because they have costs
long before they have revenue, let alone gross profitâââlet alone income.
This is by design:  profitability is a lagging indicator of product/market fit
(the adventure in venture capital is investing long before the business has
materialized!).
So this was a big milestone, and on top of that (and contrary to
Russ Hannemanâs admonition!), we
have a very large order backlog.  On the one hand, itâs truly extraordinary
to watch the business generate cash, but with demand far exceeding supply,
significant cash is needed to secure inventory and fulfill these orders.  This
is one of the peculiar dynamics of a rapidly growing hardware business: we must
commit substantial cash to components and manufacturing well before the
resulting systems reach customers.
Now, between our
Series B,
Series C, our existing debt
facilities, and the cash generated by the business itself, we felt confident we
could satisfy our current backlog, but the absolute numbers are large
enough that we would have had to exercise real caution in accepting
additional demand.
We are, after all, children of the Dot Com bustâââcautious by natureâââand
would not put the business in a position where new supply disruptions, economic
shocks, or other events outside our control could jeopardize it.
Fortunately, this is the problem that capital is born to solve.
Our investors saw this too:  they have been extraordinary believers in
Oxide from the beginning, and, thrilled by the burgeoning demand,
they wanted to be sure that we were properly positioned to fully take
advantage of Oxideâs large market.
As they have so many times for us over the seven years of the company,
Eclipse stood up to lead, and our $445M Series

together. Existing investors USIT,

Friends and Family Capital and

We are deeply appreciative of the support of these existing investors, but
we also wanted to
add new investors to the company. While there was a lot of outside interest
(profitability being the ultimate VC aphrodisiac!), there was one firm that
stood above the rest: Atreides Management, which
first got to know us nearly two years ago and has stayed close to the company
ever since.  We love their analytical approach, their courage in hard-tech
investingâââand their belief in Oxide.
Finally, we are thrilled to welcome AMD as a new strategic investor.  AMD has
long been a believer in Oxide:  it was with their support that we were able to
achieve breakthroughs like our own
platform enablement software.  And of course, we have been big believers in
AMD: one of our first big bets was
on AMD EPYC.  We think the partnership between AMD and Oxide is a lasting
one, and itâs fitting to have that expressed in the cap table.
All of these investors areâââlike usâââin it for the long haul.  When we
raised our $200M Series C, we
said that it was to entirely de-risk the company with respect to capital, to
assure both our longevity and our independence.  This Series D builds
on that assurance to allow us
to satisfy our substantial backlog while continuing to accept new demand,
expand manufacturing capacity, and invest for the enduring company that
we have always set out to build.
To our investors, partners, fans, long-time listeners, employees, and most of all customers:  thank you.
While this is but a milestone on the long road to what we know Oxide will be,
it is nonetheless an exhilarating one!"
51,2026-10-09,"By

, 


      Ricky Mulvey
    

What do influencing, AI and health sciences have in common? They're all degree programs colleges have launched in the last few years to attract students looking for a new career path. The Indicator explores how colleges are rethinking degree programs for a changing job market.
STEVE INSKEEP, HOST:  Colleges are adding some new majors to attract new students and maybe adapt to a changing job market. Here are our colleagues, Wailin Wong and Ricky Mulvey at the Indicator from Planet Money.WAILIN WONG, BYLINE: Eddie Hernandez is a professor in human anatomy and physiology at UT San Antonio. His students call him Dr. H, so we will, too. Dr. H said conversations about a new health sciences degree program started a few years ago.EDDIE HERNANDEZ: We have a fantastic biology degree, but it seemed a little broad, and it also seemed like it didn't reflect our specific population of students.RICKEY MULVEY, BYLINE: Students like Case Brown (ph). A few months ago, Dr. H told Case, guess what? The college has this new major. It checks off all the pre-reqs that you need for medical school. Plus, you'll get more tailored courses that will prepare you for an actual career as a doctor.CASE BROWN: Dr. H came to me like, hey, Case, this is new. This is how it'd benefit you. I was like, cool, sign me up.MULVEY: Dr. H says by his last count, there are around 500 undergraduates in the current cohort.WONG: And he says that if these future doctors and physicians assistants stay in Texas to build their careers, that will be another win for the program.MULVEY: And sustainable careers are the ultimate goal of these new degrees. That puts programs without a clear pathway to a job on the chopping block.WONG: The last decade or so has already been hard for the liberal arts in the U.S. The number of bachelor's degrees awarded in humanities is down 30% since 2012. That's according to the American Academy of Arts and Sciences.MULVEY: Jason Fox is the chief academic officer at the Savannah College of Art and Design in Savannah, Georgia. Jason's college has art right there in its name, and yet it still has to make tough calls about what programs to keep. This year, the college stopped accepting new enrollees in sculpture. Jason says the decisions come down to jobs and earnings potential.JASON FOX: These are economic decisions, as much as they were - either A, we weren't delivering on the promise, or B, there was no promise to deliver on because there was no market out there for it.WONG: On the flip side, Jason and other administrators at SCAD realized a few years ago that there would be a huge market for college graduates with training in AI.MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. Nvidia came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question - OK, what am I going to do next? And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers. Now, that's an interesting term. But it makes sense given the escalating cost of higher education. Students and their families want a monetary return on investment, and SCAD isn't alone in adding AI to its roster of degrees. There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new Department of Digital Humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers. Now, that's an interesting term. But it makes sense given the escalating cost of higher education. Students and their families want a monetary return on investment, and SCAD isn't alone in adding AI to its roster of degrees. There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new Department of Digital Humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

Dr. H said conversations about a new health sciences degree program started a few years ago.EDDIE HERNANDEZ: We have a fantastic biology degree, but it seemed a little broad, and it also seemed like it didn't reflect our specific population of students.RICKEY MULVEY, BYLINE: Students like Case Brown (ph). A few months ago, Dr. H told Case, guess what? The college has this new major. It checks off all the pre-reqs that you need for medical school. Plus, you'll get more tailored courses that will prepare you for an actual career as a doctor.CASE BROWN: Dr. H came to me like, hey, Case, this is new. This is how it'd benefit you. I was like, cool, sign me up.MULVEY: Dr. H says by his last count, there are around 500 undergraduates in the current cohort.WONG: And he says that if these future doctors and physicians assistants stay in Texas to build their careers, that will be another win for the program.MULVEY: And sustainable careers are the ultimate goal of these new degrees. That puts programs without a clear pathway to a job on the chopping block.WONG: The last decade or so has already been hard for the liberal arts in the U.S. The number of bachelor's degrees awarded in humanities is down 30% since 2012. That's according to the American Academy of Arts and Sciences.MULVEY: Jason Fox is the chief academic officer at the Savannah College of Art and Design in Savannah, Georgia. Jason's college has art right there in its name, and yet it still has to make tough calls about what programs to keep. This year, the college stopped accepting new enrollees in sculpture. Jason says the decisions come down to jobs and earnings potential.JASON FOX: These are economic decisions, as much as they were - either A, we weren't delivering on the promise, or B, there was no promise to deliver on because there was no market out there for it.WONG: On the flip side, Jason and other administrators at SCAD realized a few years ago that there would be a huge market for college graduates with training in AI.MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. Nvidia came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. Nvidia came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question - OK, what am I going to do next? And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers. Now, that's an interesting term. But it makes sense given the escalating cost of higher education. Students and their families want a monetary return on investment, and SCAD isn't alone in adding AI to its roster of degrees. There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new Department of Digital Humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

      Ricky Mulvey
    

What do influencing, AI and health sciences have in common? They're all degree programs colleges have launched in the last few years to attract students looking for a new career path. The Indicator explores how colleges are rethinking degree programs for a changing job market.
STEVE INSKEEP, HOST:  Colleges are adding some new majors to attract new students and maybe adapt to a changing job market. Here are our colleagues, Wailin Wong and Ricky Mulvey at the Indicator from Planet Money.WAILIN WONG, BYLINE: Eddie Hernandez is a professor in human anatomy and physiology at UT San Antonio. His students call him Dr. H, so we will, too. Dr. H said conversations about a new health sciences degree program started a few years ago.EDDIE HERNANDEZ: We have a fantastic biology degree, but it seemed a little broad, and it also seemed like it didn't reflect our specific population of students.RICKEY MULVEY, BYLINE: Students like Case Brown (ph). A few months ago, Dr. H told Case, guess what? The college has this new major. It checks off all the pre-reqs that you need for medical school. Plus, you'll get more tailored courses that will prepare you for an actual career as a doctor.CASE BROWN: Dr. H came to me like, hey, Case, this is new. This is how it'd benefit you. I was like, cool, sign me up.MULVEY: Dr. H says by his last count, there are around 500 undergraduates in the current cohort.WONG: And he says that if these future doctors and physicians assistants stay in Texas to build their careers, that will be another win for the program.MULVEY: And sustainable careers are the ultimate goal of these new degrees. That puts programs without a clear pathway to a job on the chopping block.WONG: The last decade or so has already been hard for the liberal arts in the U.S. The number of bachelor's degrees awarded in humanities is down 30% since 2012. That's according to the American Academy of Arts and Sciences.MULVEY: Jason Fox is the chief academic officer at the Savannah College of Art and Design in Savannah, Georgia. Jason's college has art right there in its name, and yet it still has to make tough calls about what programs to keep. This year, the college stopped accepting new enrollees in sculpture. Jason says the decisions come down to jobs and earnings potential.JASON FOX: These are economic decisions, as much as they were - either A, we weren't delivering on the promise, or B, there was no promise to deliver on because there was no market out there for it.WONG: On the flip side, Jason and other administrators at SCAD realized a few years ago that there would be a huge market for college graduates with training in AI.MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. Nvidia came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question - OK, what am I going to do next? And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers. Now, that's an interesting term. But it makes sense given the escalating cost of higher education. Students and their families want a monetary return on investment, and SCAD isn't alone in adding AI to its roster of degrees. There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new Department of Digital Humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

There are around 80 AI majors being offered in the U.S. right now, according to a tracker from Northeastern University.WONG: But all is not lost for the liberal arts. UCLA has a new Department of Digital Humanities. The school says students will use digital tools to explore what it means to be human.MULVEY: Ricky Mulvey.WONG: Wailin Wong, NPR News.(SOUNDBITE OF MUSIC)

WAILIN WONG, BYLINE: Eddie Hernandez is a professor in human anatomy and physiology at UT San Antonio. His students call him Dr. H, so we will, too. Dr. H said conversations about a new health sciences degree program started a few years ago.EDDIE HERNANDEZ: We have a fantastic biology degree, but it seemed a little broad, and it also seemed like it didn't reflect our specific population of students.RICKEY MULVEY, BYLINE: Students like Case Brown (ph). A few months ago, Dr. H told Case, guess what? The college has this new major. It checks off all the pre-reqs that you need for medical school. Plus, you'll get more tailored courses that will prepare you for an actual career as a doctor.CASE BROWN: Dr. H came to me like, hey, Case, this is new. This is how it'd benefit you. I was like, cool, sign me up.MULVEY: Dr. H says by his last count, there are around 500 undergraduates in the current cohort.WONG: And he says that if these future doctors and physicians assistants stay in Texas to build their careers, that will be another win for the program.MULVEY: And sustainable careers are the ultimate goal of these new degrees. That puts programs without a clear pathway to a job on the chopping block.WONG: The last decade or so has already been hard for the liberal arts in the U.S. The number of bachelor's degrees awarded in humanities is down 30% since 2012. That's according to the American Academy of Arts and Sciences.MULVEY: Jason Fox is the chief academic officer at the Savannah College of Art and Design in Savannah, Georgia. Jason's college has art right there in its name, and yet it still has to make tough calls about what programs to keep. This year, the college stopped accepting new enrollees in sculpture. Jason says the decisions come down to jobs and earnings potential.JASON FOX: These are economic decisions, as much as they were - either A, we weren't delivering on the promise, or B, there was no promise to deliver on because there was no market out there for it.WONG: On the flip side, Jason and other administrators at SCAD realized a few years ago that there would be a huge market for college graduates with training in AI.MULVEY: Jason started talking to people at both SCAD and in industries like film and fashion.WONG: So in September of 2025, SCAD launched a new major in applied AI. Nvidia came on board as a corporate partner.MULVEY: The school says the program should equip students for careers in designing AI products and machines. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question - OK, what am I going to do next? And at the undergraduate level, I don't think education consumers today are comfortable with that.MULVEY: Education consumers. Now, that's an interesting term. Jason says the No. 1 metric that the college looks at is the employment rate of graduates, and it's hard to justify programs that don't lead to sustainable careers.FOX: If you're offering generalized degrees, especially in a specialty university like ours, it begs the question - OK, what am I going to do next? 55,2026-10-09,"By 


      Reshma Kirpalani
    


                Arístides Fernandez-Garcia (left) and Yasmany Moreno-De Armas were deported to the Central African Republic on July 30, 2026.
                
                    
                    Courtesy of Yasmany Moreno-De Armas/

hide caption

MIAMI — José Díaz, 85, had never asked his grand-nephew for a single thing.
But late last year, he noticed the baseball cap Arístides Fernandez-Garcia was wearing on his visit to New Jersey. He liked it.
Fernandez-Garcia promised himself that he'd give his great-uncle the cap after returning from a Miami hearing in his pending asylum case.
He never got the chance.
On Jan. 6 Fernandez-Garcia, a Cuban immigrant, was arrested by Immigration and Customs Enforcement in a targeted operation at the courthouse in Miami.
He told NPR that as he moved through a maze of detention centers across the U.S., losing 60 pounds and witnessing shocking violence, he held hard onto his promise. When his great-uncle got sick, Fernandez-Garcia called him from detention.
""I would tell him: 'Stay strong, because I have to give you the hat,'"" Fernandez-Garcia recalled. The old man would laugh.
Eight months later, Fernandez-Garcia sits in the living room of an apartment in the Central African Republic. He feels torn: guilty for giving Díaz hope, but resolved, nevertheless, to keep his promise.
""That is something I have to do in life before anything else happens to him,"" he says about his great-uncle. ""I have to give him that hat.""
Credit:  Reshma Kirpalani for NPR
Fernandez-Garcia is among the more than 25,000 people the Trump administration says it has deported to countries to which they have no ties. On Sept. 18, a federal appeals court ruled this ""third country deportation"" policy violates due process. The court said migrants weren't given enough notice or a meaningful chance to say they were afraid to be sent to another country. On Sept. 24, the Trump administration appealed that ruling to the Supreme Court.
In a statement to NPR, the Department of Homeland Security said ""Third country removals are an essential public safety tool that remove the most dangerous criminal illegal aliens from our streets.""
But NPR found no record of criminal charges in Florida against either Fernandez-Garcia or his roommate, Yasmany Noel Moreno-De Armas, who is also a Cuban immigrant deported by the U.S. Fernandez-Garcia didn't have a deportation order either. Both men say they don't feel safe returning to Cuba.
""I face punishment from the Cuban regime for having been a balsero — someone who fled Cuba by raft — and for trying to avail myself of the 'wet foot, dry foot' policy and enter the United States under that protection,"" said Moreno-De Armas.
DHS says people being deported to third countries receive ""notice and assurances prior to their removal."" DHS added that ICE obtains diplomatic assurances from destination countries that once people are deported they will be safe after they arrive.
But both men said they didn't know where they were going when they boarded the plane in Texas. From his shared apartment in the Central African Republic, Fernandez-Garcia showed NPR how he sat on the plane for 12 hours, his hands shackled together and chained to a belt around his waist. It wasn't until they landed and he looked out the window, he says, that he saw a flag. It was striped green, yellow and red, with a green star in the center. That's the flag of Senegal, he realized. They were in Africa.
""My mind just went blank,"" said Fernandez-Garcia. ""I went blank because I realized that my life was out of my hands, like I didn't have control over my own life anymore. I still don't.""
The men say they don't feel safe in the Central African Republic, a country the State Department warns Americans not to visit, citing risks of kidnapping, landmines and terrorism. The International Organization for Migration, a group affiliated with the United Nations, is providing protection for the deportees. Armed guards accompany them whenever they leave the complex. The IOM is also providing housing in Bangui, the capital of the Central African Republic. The deportees live four to an apartment. Fernandez-Garcia and Moreno-De Armas share a bedroom.
Their building neighbors a small market: low-slung buildings with concrete walls and corrugated-metal roofs. The road is unpaved and often muddy. They shop there twice a week for canned goods and water. When the power is on, the men talk to their families on WhatsApp, do interviews with reporters and make social media videos, trying to get their story out. Maybe that will help them return to the U.S., they think. Sometimes, viewers donate money. It helps them buy supplies for their shared apartment and minutes on their phones.
But two months after arriving, both men remain in limbo, unsure of their legal status or what comes next.
""We don't have documents that would allow us to work. They haven't given us a visa,"" said Moreno-De Armas. ""We were deported from the United States to a third country and we're still detained.""
Both men have gotten sick in Bangui. Fernandez-Garcia with malaria, which kept him weak in bed for days. Moreno-De Armas with high blood pressure, shortness of breath, and panic attacks when he heard his mother was in the hospital.
But what affects them most isn't the physical toll. It's time.
""Time with family, time with those people who have less time left, no, no, that can never be recovered again,"" said Fernandez-Garcia. ""Not even with millions of dollars can that be recovered or bought.""
The men know other ""third country deportees"" who have been in Africa for a long time. Like Roberto Mosquera, who has been imprisoned in Eswatini for over a year.
""This is all I do. Walk outside. Come back in. Fix some food. Lay down. Think,"" said Mosquera from his prison cell in Matsapha Correctional Complex, a high security prison. ""You think about your kids, that life that you had before, the life that they took from you.""
Mosquera has a criminal record and has already served his time in the U.S. But according to his lawyer, Alma David, he has no criminal charges in Eswatini. Mosquera also doesn't know if and when he'll be released. Since he's been imprisoned, his oldest sister died in America. So did his best friend. He's also going to miss his daughter's wedding.
""My youngest one's going to be the flower girl,"" said Mosquera. He looks away from the screen and sighs. ""It's rough. I didn't expect this to happen at my age.""
When de Moreno-De Armas' father got a call from his son that he had been deported to Africa, he felt like ""the world fell on top of me,"" he says. Hiovany Moreno Noa sits in the backyard of his home in South Florida. Thick worry lines stretch across his forehead.
""If at least he were in a Spanish-speaking country, with more or less the same customs as us, then we could be a little more at ease,"" he says. ""But there, we don't know anything. The little we do know is that it's a country that has been at war. It's a very dangerous country.""
Credit:  Reshma Kirpalani for NPR
Moreno Noa tries to talk to his son every day. He sends what money he can though Western Union. He prays for him. But the separation is hard.
After Moreno-De Armas fled Cuba on a raft, years passed before he saw his family. They reunited in 2022.
""We went six years without seeing each other. Then we came to this country, and now we're being separated again,"" he said. ""Every parent longs to have their children beside them. And that's why we suffer.""
On Sept. 29, the Supreme Court put the appeal court's ruling on hold, clearing the way for the Trump administration to continue third-country deportations while the legal battle plays out.
In Eswatini, Mosquera still dreams about the day he can leave Africa and return to his family. In the Central African Republic, Moreno-De Armas hasn't given up hope either, despite a recent curfew that kept him and his roommates indoors for three days.
""We're waiting for God to give us the opportunity to return to the United States,"" he says.
Fernandez-Garcia is not so sure. Hope exists, he says. But it disappears very easily.
He still loves America.
""I felt what freedom was like for the first time in my life. I felt what it was to dream for the first time in my life, to leave the Cuban bubble of the dictatorship,"" Fernandez-Garcia said about the U.S. ""You can't judge a country by one particular moment.""
But then he thinks about his great-uncle in New Jersey, who lives with his great-aunt. Like grandparents, they had taken care of him in his youth. Now, he thought, it was his turn to take care of them, help them feel less lonely. He had plans. Walking with them in Central Park. Watching the sun reflect on the surface of the Jersey Shore.
""They are older people, and I feel that I have little time left with them,"" said Fernandez-Garcia, choking back tears. ""And maybe I'm not going to be there when they pass away.""
At her home in New Jersey, Martha Piedra grieves for her great-nephew.
""I'm an old woman of 79 years with a whole lot of illnesses, so just imagine. What am I going to be able to do? I can't do anything,"" she says, her words dissolving into tears.
On the phone, his great-uncle's voice trembles. He remembers Fernandez-Garcia's cap and his calls from detention. He remembers the promises his grand-nephew made to gift it to him: a black baseball cap with a white Dodge Ram logo on it. Simple, but impossible to find elsewhere.
""I hope that he comes and brings it to me someday,"" says Díaz.
Piedra corrects him. ""No, not someday. Please,"" she says. ""Let it be soon.""

09 October 2026


Apple iOS

According to a new report, Apple has asked some of its suppliers to reduce production of components for the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro Max.
The report, citing people familiar with the matter, claims that Apple cut component orders by up to 15% for October. The reduction is reportedly driven by weaker market demand amid soaring memory prices.
Meanwhile, the report also notes that demand for the new iPhones has been weaker than for last year’s models. However, another recent report painted a different picture in China, claiming that the iPhone 18 Pro models outperformed their predecessors in the market.
However, the report suggests that lower shipment volumes may not necessarily hurt Apple’s revenue, as the iPhone 18 Pro models are priced 10 percent higher than their predecessors. The iPhone 18 Pro starts at $1,199 in the US, while the iPhone 18 Pro Max has a starting price of $1,299.

57,2026-10-09,"""Price sensitivity is becoming a real problem.""
PS5 and Xbox Series X/S consoles are more expensive than ever in most markets, thanks largely to memory components being gobbled up by AI data centres. Now, analyst Mat Piscatella worries the console market, at least in the US, hasn't looked this dire since the early 1980s, which was maybe not-so-coincidentally when the industry absolutely collapsed.
Circana's Piscatella recently jumped online to reveal that Xbox and PS5 price hikes caused a big blow to sales, somewhat unsurprisingly. Xbox hardware sales are reportedly down 33% ""versus a year ago in the US, while PlayStation hardware units are 25% lower."" Those year-to-date stats mark a new all-time low for Xbox, and PlayStation's lowest point since 2013.
""The average price a consumer has paid for a new Xbox console in 2026 YTD ending August is $529, 26% higher than a year ago, while the average PlayStation console has sold for $597, 20% higher than last year,"" Piscatella added. ""Both prices are at all-time US highs. Price sensitivity is becoming a real problem.""
There's been some hope the incoming and meteoric launch of GTA 6 next month could give both consoles a boost, and Piscatella also noted that Rockstar's mammoth might ""help ease at least some of these declines,"" but that's all dependent on pricing and ""product availability."" If all the RAM is booked for AI data centres, Microsoft and Sony won't be able to make as many consoles as they can sell.
""The US hardware market has not been in a more precarious position since the early 80s,"" Piscatella added.
Consoles aren't the only devices suffering from RAMaggedon, though. Almost every consumer electronic device has been subject to price increases the past few years. The PS5, Switch 2 and Xbox Series X/S price tags have all been bumped up, sometimes multiple times, as well.
Micron's CEO recently warned that memory shortages are set to persist until at least 2028. Xbox boss Asha Sharma also delivered a similar note months earlier: ""When I joined as CEO in February, the price we paid for console storage components was over 2x as high as we paid last fall. These costs have since doubled again. And as we plan for the 2027 holiday season, we expect another significant increase, taking us over 5x the prices we paid only two years earlier.""

Black Bear has acquired domestic rights to Honeymoon / Funeral, an MRC rom-com starring Rebecca Ferguson, Greta Lee and Carrie Coon that wrapped production in September.

	Sources said the project is one of a handful to have been successfully shopped at the newly created TIFF market, alongside Josh Giuliano’s horror flick River and the Michelle Yeoh starrer The Surgeon.

	Directed by two-time Emmy nominee Nahnatchka Khan, Honeymoon / Funeral follows two women (Ferguson and Lee) who meet on a flight from LA to London – one en route to her honeymoon by herself after being left at the altar by her girlfriend, the other headed home for her mother’s funeral – and wind up spending the week together.

59,2026-10-09,"

      Business
  



      Business
  

NEW YORK, Oct 9  : The US bull market is going strong as it nears its four-year anniversary, driven by an AI spending engine that is propelling corporate profits and economic growth.
The S&P 500 is trading around record-high levels ahead of October 12, which will mark four years since the benchmark stock index's closing low for this cycle — signaling the start of the latest bull run.
Sizzling corporate profit growth — lifted by spending on the AI expansion and a solid economic backdrop — has helped drive the latest leg of the market's rally and is a primary reason for investor optimism in coming quarters. 
But even as the index climbs, risks loom. The Federal Reserve's interest rate hikes and spiking US Treasury yields both provide obstacles that could sap the momentum for equities. Markets could be volatile heading into next month's US midterm elections.

The market's dependence on AI also presents a worry, with any hints of weakness in the trend possibly met with severe punishment. ""The AI theme is the defining feature of this bull market,"" said Anthony Saglimbene, chief market strategist at Ameriprise. ""What you're seeing in terms of the bull market four years in is, I think, the easy money around AI has been made ... as we get further into this bull market, there is just going to be more pressure on especially technology companies to prove that the spending that they're doing today is actually going to translate into the profits.""CURRENT RUN RANKS MIDDLE-AGED AMONG BULLSAs bull markets go, the current one could be classified as middle-aged. The S&P 500's latest run ranks as the eighth-longest bull market since World War Two, according to Ryan Detrick, chief market strategist at Carson Group.While stock experts differ on defining a bull market, a common definition is a gain of at least 20 per cent that has followed a decline of at least 20 per cent from a peak.The current bull run has tallied a gain of 117 per cent, which is the sixth-best-performing bull market since World War Two. ""Four years is not, by any stretch of the imagination, scary with regards to a bull market,"" said Mark Hackett, chief market strategist for Nationwide. ""They don't end of old age; they end from disease.""TECH, AI AT HEART OF BULL RUNAI has dominated the latest bull run, with the launch of ChatGPT coming about a month after the bull market began. US companies are posting massive profit growth, with S&P 500 earnings expected to rise more than 35 per cent this year, boosted by capital spending from ""hyperscalers"" to build data centers.Oxford Economics estimates about one-third of recent US economic growth stems from AI, including the net impact of direct investment to support expansion of AI infrastructure as well as some contribution from the wealth effect from stock market gains lifting consumer spending.""You're seeing that AI theme show up in the economy and in corporate profits,"" Saglimbene said.Of the 11 S&P 500 sectors, only technology and communication services — which includes megacap AI players Alphabet and Meta Platforms — have posted stronger gains than the index itself during the bull run.The market capitalization of Nvidia — whose AI chips have made the company the poster child of this technological era — has soared to $5.8 trillion from $286 billion on October 12, 2022. It has become the largest company by market value in the world. Thirteen US companies boast market values of at least $1 trillion — all but two either in the tech sector or with significant AI exposure.CONCENTRATION RISK GROWS WITH TECH DOMINANCEThe gains in massive tech and AI stocks give them more significant sway over major stock indexes, but also make them top-heavy. The weight of the top 10 companies in the S&P 500 has grown to about 40 per cent from about 28 per cent in October 2022, according to J.P. Morgan Asset Management.""It is a reflection of fundamental strength and earnings outperformance, but also it introduces some risks,"" said Angelo Kourkafas, senior global investment strategist at Edward Jones. ""The risk of concentration is that if the prevailing theme goes out of favor, portfolios might feel it in an outsized way.""Another risk is the Fed's recent pivot to rate hikes, as the US central bank seeks to bring down high inflation. Tighter monetary policy could slow the economy, perhaps severely. Indeed, the last bear market that led to the low in October 2022 coincided with a sharp rate-hiking cycle.Rate hikes also factor in to the huge jump in Treasury yields. The benchmark 10-year Treasury yield was hovering at around 5.2 per cent, after recently reaching its highest level in 24 years. Higher yields pose headwinds for equities, including potentially greater investment competition from bonds.Kourkafas said Edward Jones remains overweight equities but the recommendation is less aggressive than it was before, with the strategist noting the increasing attractiveness of fixed income.""We still think that the bull market is not about to end ... but it makes sense to us to take some of the risk off the table,"" he said.
The market's dependence on AI also presents a worry, with any hints of weakness in the trend possibly met with severe punishment. 
""The AI theme is the defining feature of this bull market,"" said Anthony Saglimbene, chief market strategist at Ameriprise. ""What you're seeing in terms of the bull market four years in is, I think, the easy money around AI has been made ... as we get further into this bull market, there is just going to be more pressure on especially technology companies to prove that the spending that they're doing today is actually going to translate into the profits.""
CURRENT RUN RANKS MIDDLE-AGED AMONG BULLS
As bull markets go, the current one could be classified as middle-aged. The S&P 500's latest run ranks as the eighth-longest bull market since World War Two, according to Ryan Detrick, chief market strategist at Carson Group.
While stock experts differ on defining a bull market, a common definition is a gain of at least 20 per cent that has followed a decline of at least 20 per cent from a peak.
The current bull run has tallied a gain of 117 per cent, which is the sixth-best-performing bull market since World War Two. 
""Four years is not, by any stretch of the imagination, scary with regards to a bull market,"" said Mark Hackett, chief market strategist for Nationwide. ""They don't end of old age; they end from disease.""
TECH, AI AT HEART OF BULL RUN
AI has dominated the latest bull run, with the launch of ChatGPT coming about a month after the bull market began. US companies are posting massive profit growth, with S&P 500 earnings expected to rise more than 35 per cent this year, boosted by capital spending from ""hyperscalers"" to build data centers.
Oxford Economics estimates about one-third of recent US economic growth stems from AI, including the net impact of direct investment to support expansion of AI infrastructure as well as some contribution from the wealth effect from stock market gains lifting consumer spending.
""You're seeing that AI theme show up in the economy and in corporate profits,"" Saglimbene said.
Of the 11 S&P 500 sectors, only technology and communication services — which includes megacap AI players Alphabet and Meta Platforms — have posted stronger gains than the index itself during the bull run.
The market capitalization of Nvidia — whose AI chips have made the company the poster child of this technological era — has soared to $5.8 trillion from $286 billion on October 12, 2022. It has become the largest company by market value in the world. Thirteen US companies boast market values of at least $1 trillion — all but two either in the tech sector or with significant AI exposure.
CONCENTRATION RISK GROWS WITH TECH DOMINANCE
The gains in massive tech and AI stocks give them more significant sway over major stock indexes, but also make them top-heavy. The weight of the top 10 companies in the S&P 500 has grown to about 40 per cent from about 28 per cent in October 2022, according to J.P. Morgan Asset Management.
""It is a reflection of fundamental strength and earnings outperformance, but also it introduces some risks,"" said Angelo Kourkafas, senior global investment strategist at Edward Jones. ""The risk of concentration is that if the prevailing theme goes out of favor, portfolios might feel it in an outsized way.""
Another risk is the Fed's recent pivot to rate hikes, as the US central bank seeks to bring down high inflation. Tighter monetary policy could slow the economy, perhaps severely. Indeed, the last bear market that led to the low in October 2022 coincided with a sharp rate-hiking cycle.
Rate hikes also factor in to the huge jump in Treasury yields. The benchmark 10-year Treasury yield was hovering at around 5.2 per cent, after recently reaching its highest level in 24 years. 
Higher yields pose headwinds for equities, including potentially greater investment competition from bonds.
Kourkafas said Edward Jones remains overweight equities but the recommendation is less aggressive than it was before, with the strategist noting the increasing attractiveness of fixed income.
""We still think that the bull market is not about to end ... but it makes sense to us to take some of the risk off the table,"" he said.

					By Peter White 
Executive Editor, Television

EXCLUSIVE: Stolen Kingdom, Joshua Bailey’s documentary about the lucrative black market for stolen props at Walt Disney World, is heading to Mipcom after BayView Entertainment acquired the international rights.

	The company behind Skinamarink will shop the film to international broadcasters and streamers at next week’s international TV trade convention. It comes six months after the doc, premiered theatrically six months after following a festival run that saw it premiere at Big Sky and feature at Slamdance, IFF Boston and the Florida Film Festival.

Stolen Kingdom delves into the history of mischief, scandal, and theft at Walt Disney World ultimately leading to the theft of an animatronic valued at nearly half a million dollars. It centers on the infamous 2018 heist of “Buzzy” from Epcot and covers the urban explorers that chase abandoned locations at the theme park.

61,2026-10-09,"Every family treats their newly adult children differently. I remember turning 18 but still living at home; I didn't have to ask my parents for as much permission to hang out with my friends. Sure, I was technically an adult, but not much changed in my life from being a 17-year-old junior in high school to an 18-year-old senior. If my parents wanted to, they could've reminded me that they paid for my food, car, and housing every time I wanted to do something they didn't want me to. They could've said, ""If you live under my roof, you'll live by my rules,"" but they didn't. They understood that treating me like I was still a child when I could legally do whatever I wanted was going to hurt our relationship with each other. Not to mention, that's just not their style!
When I went to college, I was so ready to live independently that I didn't come home for the entire first semester. While I had some moments when I reached out to my parents for emotional support, I felt like I was functioning pretty independently. I didn't need (or want) my parents to wake me up in the morning, remind me to do my homework, or make sure I ate properly. I had to learn how to function on my own. One big lesson I learned my freshman year was that if I ate multiple bowls of Cinnamon Toast Crunch for breakfast every morning at the dining hall, I was going to gain weight! Who knew? 
While I felt independent, objectively, I wasn't functioning completely on my own. I was totally financially dependent on my parents, even though I was an adult. A pretty sizable portion of Americans want their kids out of their house and financially independent by the time they graduate high school, and that was never my parents' vibe. As I see it, as long as an adult child is getting an education to prepare them for the job market, their parents shouldn't leave them high and dry. 
Every parenting approach has pros and cons, and I'm grateful my parents aren't treating me like I'm a teenager, even though I'm 27. If you find yourself in that situation, whether you're a college student or a working young adult, Psychology Today reported tips to help young people talk to overbearing parents about giving them space. Although a serious conversation with your parents can help, sometimes parents don't want to listen to their adult children. Sometimes, another adult telling them that they're being too overbearing is a more effective way to change their behavior. 
A college student. 
Model representation. 
I don't understand why making appointments, of all things, is so difficult for my fellow Gen Zers. I know a lot of people have phone anxiety, but I don't remember the last time I had to call my doctor's office or my hairdresser. Booking is all online, and it's so easy! I think some of these parents just like to feel useful, and that's why they make their kids' appointments.

62,2026-10-09,"TOPICS

                    off-prem
                

        UPDATED
    

        Putin this cloud region back into operation might not be possible, says Yandex
    
Russian web giant Yandex says one of its cloud availability zones is unavailable due to a drone attack.
Yandex is Russia’s rough equivalent of Google, and dominates the local market for search, email, and mapping services. The company also operates a cloud computing service whose status page says its ru-central1-b availability zone is down.
“The incident was caused by a fire caused by a drone attack on Yandex's data center in Sasovo, which affected part of the Yandex Cloud infrastructure. Data center operations have been completely halted.” Sasovo is a town around 300 kilometers southeast of Moscow.
Yandex later recommended users employ unspecified “alternative service restoration plans,” including its own bare metal servers, but advised users “it will not be possible to restore service in the near future.”
The Russian company hasn’t commented on the source of the drone attack, but Ukrainian press report that President Volodymyr Zelensky has said Russia has attacked Ukrainian datacenters and that his government always responds in kind.
Reuters reports that Yandex is uncertain if it is possible to restore operations at the datacenter.
If Yandex must write off the datacenter, it will be the second cloud provider to lose facilities to drone attacks this year after AWS decided its Bahrain facility could not be recovered after an Iranian drone attack.
Iran hit AWS facilities because it believed the cloud giant hosts workloads for the US military and its suppliers, and that an attack could therefore disrupt hostilities.
For years, industry execs have told The Register that datacenters are critical infrastructure and will therefore likely become a target for kinetic attacks during conflict – either directly or by attacking their connections to energy grids and communications services. Clouds and pundits alike advise using multiple geographically dispersed availability zones, and cloud suppliers, to improve resilience.
That advice is a little less potent in Russia, where sanctions mean local businesses aren’t welcome in clouds across the border. Russia also has a smaller pool of cloud operators to choose from. ®Updated to add at 1230 UTC:Yandex has just confirmed a second datacenter has been struck. Looks like Ukraine is going after more critical infrastructure.

63,2026-10-09,"Elon Musk (right) has accused the Ambanis of blocking Starlink's entry into India 
Elon Musk has questioned whether billionaire Mukesh Ambani is the ""real boss of India"" as his satellite internet company Starlink struggles to secure final approval to launch in the country.
The remark came a day after Musk accused unnamed powerful business interests of trying to block Starlink's entry into India. He then singled out Ambani, whose Reliance conglomerate owns Jio, India's biggest mobile network operator.
India has rejected Musk's allegations, saying its satellite communications rules are fair and non-discriminatory.
Ambani and Reliance Group have not responded to Musk's remarks. The BBC has reached out to them for comment
The row comes as competition grows in India's satellite internet market, with Reliance Jio and Bharti Airtel also preparing to launch services.
The rivalry is complicated by the fact that Starlink has also partnered with Ambani's Reliance Jio and Bharti Airtel. 
In March 2025, both companies signed agreements with SpaceX to distribute Starlink equipment and services in India, subject to government approval. The companies also said they could explore sharing network infrastructure. The BBC has also reached out to Bharti Airtel for comment. 
Musk first made the allegations on Wednesday, accusing ""certain oligarchs"" of maintaining a ""monopolistic chokehold"" on Indian consumers.
""You can guess who they are,"" he wrote on X, external, adding that blocking Starlink was ""a crime against the people of India"".
On Thursday, India's Ministry of Communications responded, saying that three operators - Starlink, Eutelsat OneWeb and Jio Satellite Communications - were at broadly the same stage of the approval process, with all three awaiting security clearances and spectrum allocation before they can launch commercial services.
""The suggestion that the framework or its application is unfair or discriminatory is baseless and misconceived,"" the ministry said, without naming Musk directly.
Musk continued to question the delay, saying Starlink was already licensed in more than 165 countries.
""Starlink is licensed in over 165 countries and has spent five years complying with every single law and requirement of the government of India, so why still no license? Is Ambani the real boss of India?"" he wrote on X on Thursday.
Starlink, owned by Musk's SpaceX, uses satellites in low Earth orbit to provide internet access, particularly in remote areas where mobile networks and broadband cables are difficult or expensive to install.
The company has been trying to enter India since 2021 and has secured key licences. But it still needs final security clearances and spectrum allocation before it can begin commercial services.
Mukesh Ambani, who owns Reliance, is the richest man in Asia 
Wednesday's row unfolded as senior executives from Starlink, Reliance Jio and Bharti Airtel gathered at the India Mobile Congress in Delhi, where all three companies discussed their plans for satellite internet services.
Speaking at the event, Akash Ambani, chairman of Reliance Jio Infocomm and Mukesh Ambani's son, said the company was developing its own satellite communications technology and working towards regulatory approvals.
Asked by a reporter about Musk's allegations, Akash declined to comment and only said, ""We want to be heads down and deliver value to our Indian consumers.""    
Bharti Airtel chairman Sunil Bharti Mittal said his company's satellite venture was also awaiting government approvals.
SpaceX executive Lauren Dreyer, who oversees Starlink's business operations, said the company had spent years working with Indian regulators and had built security controls into its network to meet the country's requirements.
Security has been a key concern surrounding Starlink's entry into India. In 2024, Indian authorities said they had recovered Starlink equipment during a drug-smuggling investigation in the remote Andaman and Nicobar Islands and an army operation in the northeastern state of Manipur.
Musk said at that time that Starlink's service had never been active in India, arguing that the equipment recovered by authorities could not have been using its satellite network.
Questions also remain over how affordable the service would be for Indian consumers, particularly those in rural areas.
India is one of the world's largest internet markets but access remains uneven, particularly in remote areas where mobile networks and broadband cables are difficult or expensive to build.
Musk has argued that Starlink could help bridge this gap. ""Starlink in India would enable high-speed, affordable internet connectivity for those who can't afford current prices or who don't have a connection at all!"" he wrote on X on Wednesday. 
The company has yet to announce prices for India, but some experts say its plans elsewhere suggest the service could be beyond the reach of many Indian households.
In the US, Starlink's residential plans start at $55 a month, although prices and equipment costs vary by plan and location.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.
